## Global Data Watch

0 Strong flash DM PMI bolsters confidence in a broader growth base

• Revising up Euro area growth, 2%ar consumption gain expected

ECB, BoC set for June ease; Risks to Aug BoE ease shift to later

0 Next week: SA elections; firm US core PCE; modest EA core HICP

## Boosting the base

Surveys of firms and households play a central role in our tracking of the global business cycle. Although not direct inputs into activity and inflation measures, they provide the earliest signal of performance each month on a consistent basis across the globe. Global survey measures of consumer confidence, manufacturing expectations (MEI), and global purchasing managers (PMI) are thus incorporated in our suite of daily nowcasters and our ongoing assessment of forecast risk. The relationship between surveys and activity has, however, weakened post-pandemic. While the value of a diffusion index was understood to be limited through the high volatility in sectoral demand in the early stages of the pandemic shock, the persistent outperformance of DM GDP vis à vis PMI readings is harder to explain (Figure 1). The relationship between consumer confidence and spending has also weakened (Figure 2). While our recent analysis points to the emergence of a “malaise<sup>”</sup> in which DM confidence readings have broadly underperformed household macroeconomic fundamentals, this malaise has not tracked either aggregate or relative consumption outcomes.

Against this backdrop we interpret this week<sup>’</sup>s significant DM flash PMI upside surprise. The May composite output PMI rose a larger-than-expected 1.6-pts, with broad-based improvements across sectors and regions. The output PMI is now up for six consecutive months and stands at its strongest level in a year (53.3). However, given the survey<sup>’</sup>s recent volatility and limited accuracy, we downplay its signal on GDP growth momentum. Indeed, it continues to track below our 2Q24 DM GDP forecast, an outcome that would be consistent with a moderation in overall global growth.

Figure 1: DM all-industry PMI and real GDP DI, sa %q/q, saar; 2Q24 forecast boxed

We would place greater emphasis on the survey<sup>’</sup>s signal about breadth:

Euro area up, US not down. The PMI is most reliable in tracking the Euro area and the May gain prompted us to raise our sights on GDP growth to 1.5%ar for this quarter and next. While US business surveys have not tracked activity well in recent years, the coincident slide in the April PMI and ISM surveys raised concern. With the May PMI bouncing and April activity readings recording solid gains, our forecast for a 2.3%ar current-quarter GDP gain remains on track.

A broadening lift in global manufacturing sector. Although global IP will be held back as China comes off the boil this quarter, the PMI suggests an anticipated recovery elsewhere is taking hold. The flash output PMI rose to its highest level in 23 months with gains registered across all countries surveyed. Overall the survey is aligned with a modest recovery in DM output growth this year, but the failure of the orders/inventory ratio to lift from depressed levels strikes a cautionary note.

Output price pressure remains moderate. The output price PMI remains elevated relative to its pre-pandemic norm, but a rise in the survey during the first three months of this year has been partially reversed with declines in April and May.

More broadly this week<sup>’</sup>s news sends a positive signal about the global industrial cycle with strong readings on exports from Taiwan and Korea aligning with a large gain in Japan machinery orders. If we are right the May global manufacturing PMI due in early June will move higher despite a slowing China and reinforce the message that both the sectoral and regional breadth of the global expansion is increasing.

Figure 3: Household saving rate
![](images/3037430f2aecb7e8b1f90f103656b7bf3d555237c2b7065cf2db02caf2782c94.jpg)

## Still waiting on Euro area consumers

Our forecast for a broadening global growth base depends on a pickup in consumer spending outside the US. Last quarter showed an EM consumption lift providing a healthy complement to still solid US spending gains. Next week, US April real spending is likely to post a 0.3%m/m increase, leaving consumption on track for a solid current-quarter gain. However, Japanese consumption contracted for a second consecutive quarter in 1Q24. With German consumers also pulling back, it now looks like the Euro area eked out a meager 0.3%ar gain last quarter. This disappointing outcome owes to a surprising 0.4%-pt jump in the Euro area saving rate that widens an already large behavioral gap with the US (Figure 3). Despite a 20% surge in incomes and 18% jump in wealth since the end of 2019, Euro area households raised their saving rate 2.5%-pts. By comparison, US households have responded to their 25% bounce in incomes and 33% jump in wealth by reducing their saving rate 3.3%-pts. These large differences help explain why US GDP is 2% above its prepandemic potential path while Euro area GDP is stuck 2% below its path.

We believe the Euro area consumer is poised to lift on the back of real income gains. This week<sup>’</sup>s 1Q24 negotiated pay report surprised on the upside, showing a 4.7%oya gain. With inflation now moving lower households are expected to have ample real income gains in the coming quarters. We forecast spending to rise at a faster than 2%ar over the coming two quarters. With this outcome expected to align with stable savings rates, any lifting of consumer malaise would likely generate an upside growth surprise.

## Sticky inflation and various CB responses

Despite some moderation in April from its elevated 1Q24 pace, the message of sticky global inflation remains intact. In relatively few countries has core inflation moved below a 3%ar (Figure 4). Despite persistent concern about elevated inflation, some additional DM central banks are set to ease in June.

Figure 4: Core CPI: April 2024 vs. 1Q24
![](images/462ce9275b255ad4a189626c3acab0750c4f8c6db6f8af9aa722eed8ed46c8d9.jpg)

ECB doubts won<sup>’</sup>t stop an ease. Firm negotiated pay and weak productivity in 1Q24 raises inflation concerns. However, a disinflationary narrative can still be pieced together. The upside surprise in the pay data came entirely from Germany, where delayed pay settlements and oneoffs are still responses to the past inflation surge. Elsewhere, pay growth has started to moderate, which is likely the more meaningful signal. And after four high monthly readings (averaging 0.30%m/m), core inflation moderated to 0.15%m/m last month. If next week<sup>’</sup>s flash inflation report for May aligns with our 0.21%m/m forecast the ECB will likely ease next month.

BoC gets the all-clear for cuts. Canada<sup>’</sup>s April inflation matched our expectations, decelerating to 2.7%oya. All three of the BoC<sup>’</sup>s core measures are now in the target range and the 3-month pace is the lowest in over three years. With inflation cooling and growth softening, we expect the Bank of Canada will be comfortable taking back some of its policy restraint next month. We look for the BoC to deliver a first rate cut at its June 5 meeting. We also continue to expect the BoC will cut its policy rate 150bp by mid-2025.

UK inflation delivered a huge upside surprise this week, driven by a services price spike. The outturn was probably exaggerated by annual price resets but it demonstrates still firm pricing power to pass on labor costs. With consumer confidence improving we expect firmer demand than the BoE this year. The odds of a June cut were further reduced by this week<sup>’</sup>s call for a July 4 election and the risks to our call for an August cut have shifted toward a later move.

## Growth and prices pick up in Japan

Several transitory factors, including the Noto earthquake and the shutdown of the auto plants, held back 1Q24 Japan growth. But the year-long weakening in consumer spending is hard to dismiss. By contrast, this week<sup>’</sup>s data sent an encouraging signal that businesses are perking back up in 2Q. The May flash PMI report showed a strong bounce in the manufacturing output index, surpassing 50 for the first time in a year, indicating fading the drag. The message is reinforced by a jump in core machinery orders, up 18.7%q/q, saar in 1Q, pointing to a recovery in capex. Coupled with solid price momentum (underscored by this week<sup>’</sup>s solid core CPI print for April), we see the BoJ on track to hike in July.

## EM Asia on hold

While Latam and CEE continue to ease, limited rate buffers in EM Asia keep central banks focused on external financing conditions. Although we see the region starting rate cuts in 4Q24, easing is contingent on the Fed and the dollar. After delivering a surprise 25bp hike last month, Bank Indonesia paused its tightening this week, citing more benign US monetary conditions. A prolonged delay in Fed easing could prompt a further hike again even as inflation remains within target. Elsewhere in the region, BoK policy is driven more by domestic growth-inflation dynamics. We changed our BoK rate call after the April meeting to look for a later and shallower easing cycle as we saw upside risks to the BoK<sup>’</sup>s GDP growth and inflation forecasts. Data since then have validated our view and were reflected in upgrades to the BoK<sup>’</sup>s macro forecasts this week, which nonetheless remain consistent with a first cut in 4Q24.

## South Africa elections to end ANC majority

South African elections on May 29 are highly contested with the governing ANC likely to lose the absolute majority it has held since 1994. Polls point to ANC support of around 45%, which would allow it to form a coalition with just one or two minor opposition parties and leave the policy outlook broadly unchanged. However, modestly lower support could require many more minor coalition partners or result in a minority government. We see chances for a tie up with the left-radical EFF or MK as very low. While risks to the currency from political developments have faded, we expect the SARB to retain a hawkish tone when it meets just after the elections. While upside inflation risks (including from El Niño) have eased and the currency has strengthened, the SARB is likely to remain hesitant to cut rates until domestic services inflation softens further and the FOMC is close to easing.

## CBRT delivers macroprudential tightening

The CBRT kept its policy rate unchanged at 50% as widely expected but delivered tightening though a series of macroprudential measures to contain financial stresses related to cross-bordering financing. The bank also reiterated its readiness to hike in the event of a significant deterioration in inflation. The CBRT hiked RRR for TRY deposits and KKM to mop up the excess lira liquidity stemming from the surging demand for Turkish lira financial assets—a move we expect will push the overnight interbank lira rate up toward 50%, from 47%. Moreover, in a bid to curb FX loan growth, the CBRT imposed a monthly 2% loan growth cap for FX credits and introduced a RRR for lending above this limit. We expect tighter fiscal policy to support a sharp fall in inflation from 75%oya in April to 43.5% at year-end.

## Expecting a Banxico June ease

This week<sup>’</sup>s Mexico activity indicator reinforces our view that last quarter<sup>’</sup>s slowdown was transitory. Notably 1Q24 GDP growth was revised up to a 1.1%ar while the March GDP and April trade reports point to strengthening momentum into the current quarter. These results are, in our view, still consistent with our forecast for a 4%ar GDP gain. Inflation news for May is mixed, and it is too early to confirm that core services inflation is on a downward path that paves the way for Banxico rate cuts. This week<sup>’</sup>s minutes confirmed the Board is split between those calling for gradual, prudent actions, and a minority of hawks calling for an extended pause. We expect a 3-2 vote in favor of a cut in June, provided core inflation continues to moderate in upcoming prints.

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our forecast for 2Q24 global real GDP growth ticked up 0.1%-pt this week to 2.4% (Figure 1). Our top-down global nowcaster ticked down to 2.3%ar, suggesting balanced risks to global growth (Table 2).

One-third of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 2Q growth in roughly half of these economies (Figure 2).

Our CapexNow model suggests a downshift in capex growth from 2.5%ar in 1Q24 to 1.5%ar in 2Q24 (Table 4).

This week<sup>’</sup>s DM flash PMIs for May were stronger than expected, with broad-based improvements across sectors and regions. Global consumer spending still looks to be firming through March. The monthly momentum in our capex nowcaster has flatlined at the start of 2Q.

% change saar; forecast by date made, black line shows potential growth

## US: Will the real current account deficit please stand up

• Over the last two years the current account deficit has narrowed from over 4.5% of GDP to under 3%

• Alternative measures, however, show little narrowing in the deficit recently

• Among several reasons for the divergence, mismeasured remittances stand out

A higher underlying deficit would also have tempered the rise in r\* in models that estimate such an increase

The current account measures a country<sup>’</sup>s transactions with the rest of the world. There are at least four ways to measure the current account. For the US, the official measure of the current account deficit has recently narrowed to 2.8% of GDP. As we discuss below, the other three measures all indicate a wider current account deficit, in the range of 3%-5% of GDP. We then mention factors that could be causing this divergence. Finally, we conclude with a few thoughts about neutral interest rates.

## One potato…

The current account (CA) measures how much a country earns from its interactions with the rest of the world. An equivalent definition is the change in a country<sup>’</sup>s holdings of net foreign assets (before revaluation effects). One major foreign source of income is export receipts, less payments for imports (X-M). A country can also earn income from domestically owned factors hosted abroad. An example of such net factor payments (NFP) is profits earned on outbound foreign domestic investments. Finally, net transfer receipts (NTR) are payments not involving economic transactions, principally foreign aid and remittances by immigrants to their home country. This is the first and customary way to measure the current account:

$$
\mathbf { C A } = \mathbf { X } - \mathbf { M } + \mathbf { N F P } - \mathbf { N T R } .
$$

The aggregate expenditure method decomposes economic output into sales for household consumption, investment, government consumption, and net exports, or:

$$
\mathrm { G D P = C + I + G + X - M } .
$$

While GDP has become the marquee measure of economic activity, for the total income a nation earns at home and abroad the relevant measure is gross national product:

$$
\mathrm { G N P = G D P + N F P - N T R } .
$$

We can also define national saving (S) as national income less consumption by both households and the government:

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathrm { S } = \mathrm { G N P - C - G } , } \end{array}
$$

Rearranging these relationships, we can see that:

$$
\mathbf { S } - \mathbf { I } = \mathbf { X } - \mathbf { M } + \mathbf { N F P } - \mathbf { N T R } = \mathbf { C A }
$$

This is the second way to measure the current account: saving less investment. Recently this measure has shown a larger deficit than the traditional measure (Figure 1), indicating a deficit of 4.8% of GDP. While a not-unprecedented difference, the gap has opened after two decades in which the two measures moved closely together.

Figure 1: The current account and saving-investment gap
![](images/c9b5d1ae36f0b032d4d8de6d2e72309de5e7b731725a587b3a6610042baa81fb.jpg)

The third measure of the current account, from the Flow of Funds, looks at the financial and capital flows that are associated with the net acquisition of foreign assets (Figure 2).

Figure 2: The current account and net lending to rest of world
![](images/9af8232a54584f4826674917ff72823f7fd7e242b84d3939b1e805278ad626da.jpg)

This measure is very volatile (note we present a four-quarter moving average), and recently shows a deficit of 3.9% of GDP. Given the volatility of the measure, the gap may not look so striking. Though note that over the prior 30 years the FOF measure generally portrayed a narrower current account deficit, on average by 0.75%-pt of GDP.

Finally, the global current account balance should be zero (barring extraterrestrial trade). So the fourth way to measure the US current account is as the negative of the global ex-US current account (Figure 3). (Note these data are from the IMF so we use their non-seasonally adjusted numbers for consistency, though this adds some choppiness to the picture.) In the last few years this measure has also been wider than the official US number, and over the last four quarters has averaged 4.7% of GDP.

Figure 3: The US and rest of world current accounts
![](images/ec1f0e520aabf24a51d378c0eea5eb2bf7a706951a8c4d107ed00e958214b6e6.jpg)

So, all three alternative measures have recently been showing a wider current account deficit than the official figure. While not proof positive of a wider deficit, it does suggest some scrutiny is warranted.

One possible source of mismeasurement comes in the net transfer receipts (NTR) line, particularly the remittances estimate. According to the BEA, these remittances are “estimated using a model that incorporates demographic and economic characteristics of the foreign-born population.<sup>”</sup> That model uses the Census Bureau<sup>’</sup>s estimates of the foreign-born population—estimates that have recently come into question.

A check on these estimates can come from inbound remittances in countries with large immigrant populations in the US. Since these remittances can be quite important for some smaller countries, more effort is made in measuring these flows, usually by their respective central banks.

My colleague Steven Palacio has collected these figures for Mexico and Central America. Of course, not all US immigrants are from Mexico and Central America, and not all remittances to Mexico and Central America are from the US. Even so, through 2017 these estimates matched US outbound remittances quite closely (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Remittances
![](images/0ad400403d0050c82892afd7143432ea9bea15adb17553133dc6b9605de7f431.jpg)

More recently, however, a gap of around \$50 billion (saar) has opened between these measures. And this may be an understatement. Data from the Border Patrol indicate that around half of the southwest land border encounters over the past two years are with people from countries outside of Mexico and Central America. Venezuela, notably, does not produce inbound remittance data.

Another area to consider is how taxes may bias the treatment of business foreign source income. Relative to a conceptually correct measure, the US<sup>’</sup>s formerly global tax system may have understated net exports. If the anti-base erosion measures in the 2017 Tax Cuts and Jobs Act (TCJA) were effective then that would have the effect of narrowing the official measure of the current account, though it<sup>’</sup>s hard to find a smoking gun in things like imports of intellectual property.

Finally, crypto and other digital assets have not been fully incorporated into US macroeconomic data (though that may change after the upcoming revision of the System of National Accounts handbook). Even so, some of the financial flows associated with crypto may be entering the source data. So this could be biasing any or all four of the measures discussed above, and in either direction. However, some preliminary work by the BEA suggests the magnitude is unlikely to account for the existing measurement gaps.

Among the many reasons to be concerned with the current account is its influence on domestic interest rates. As we discussed in prior research, running a current account deficit means that global forces are exerting downward pressure on domestic interest rates. Some models are suggesting that the neutral interest rate, or r\*, is rising. A wider current account deficit indicates that global forces are tempering this increase.

## Türkiye: Still overheated, but set to cool

• Turkish economy still appears overheated with an output gap of 1.2% in 1H24

However, broad and coordinated policy tightening is set to engineer negative output gaps starting in 3Q24

• This would support disinflation and current account adjustment to reasonable levels

• But strong domestic demand in 1H24 still pushes our GDP growth forecast to 3% (previously 2.7%)

The Turkish economy has been in a prolonged expansion above its potential since 1Q21. Between March 2021 and May 2023, Türkiye\`s policymakers prioritized economic growth over macroeconomic stability. Expansionary monetary and income policies led to an overheated economy with elevated inflation and large current account deficits during this period. In June 2023, the economic team started a macro adjustment process via rate hikes and macroprudential measures aiming to fix various imbalances, including the positive output gap. Now, in 1H24, the economy still appears overheated with the output gap at 1.2%, due to wage hikes and earthquake-related government spending. However, we believe the Turkish economy is heading towards its first negative output gap since 1Q21. Coordinated monetary and fiscal policy tightening is set to engineer a period of persistent negative gaps starting in 3Q24. This would support disinflation and current account adjustment in 2H24 and 2025.

## After a prolonged expansion...

While the presence of a positive output gap is not the main inflationary driver in Türkiye, it does amplify inflationary pressures. It is also a factor which could limit disinflation. It is thus worthwhile to measure the degree of overheating. We estimate the output gap by using a forecast-augmented HP-filter and Kalman filter monitored by CBRT (Figure 1). The trend of these two output gap estimations are similar, but their levels vary from time to time during periods of supply side shocks.

The output gap indicators carry significant uncertainty due to the unobserved nature of the potential growth. That\`s why we also compare our output gap indicator with the “surveybased<sup>”</sup> manufacturing capacity utilization rate (Figure 2). The disadvantage of the capacity utilization rate is that it does not include the services sector. That said, general trend of the capacity utilization rate is similar to that of our output gap indicator, but they differ from each other in periods when services diverge from manufacturing, as in 3Q16 due to the failed coup attempt (Figure 2).

The CBRT calculates several output gap indicators, and averages estimations (HP filter, Kalman filter, sectoral, and survey-based) in its analysis. Similar to our work, CBRT\`s output gap estimations show that the Turkish economy has had a prolonged expansion above its potential with excess domestic demand adding to supply-side inflationary pressures for 13 consecutive quarters (including 1Q24).

![](images/268f1ed16504bf165432666b4a8315779fded80ea35e886bdc11a6ebbcd0b8d9.jpg)

Figure 2: Output gap versus capacity utilization rate
![](images/3a014b4e120b3b7d00cd9f1cc81b354e500167fc71fa91b680e66baaa702a4d6.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, CBRT, TurkStat
\*seasonally adjusted data measured as deviations from 2007–2023 average (% of total manufacturing capacity)

## …still overheated...

According to our output gap estimates, the Turkish economy had been running over its potential post the Covid pandemic (i.e., between 2Q21 and 2Q24). The output gap reached as high as +2.4% in 2Q23 due to expansionary monetary and fiscal policies ahead of the May 2023 general elections (Figure 4). On the back of the CBRT\`s rate hikes, GDP growth slowed to 1.3%q/q, saar in 3Q23, from 15.2% in 2Q23. However, GDP growth reaccelerated in 4Q23 and 1Q24 due to wage hikes and increased fiscal spending (Figure 3). GDP growth ended 2023 at 4.5% suggesting an overheated economy with an output gap of 1.4% (Table 1). We expect GDP to grow by 6.1%q/q, saar (previously 4.5%) in 1Q24, from 3.9% in the previous quarter as both IP and retail sales were much stronger than in 4Q23. We also anticipate a more limited slowdown towards -0.4%q/q, saar in 2Q24 (previously -1%) as real card spending and consumption goods imports were still holding up in April and May. Hence, we revise up our GDP growth forecast to 3% for 2024 (previously 2.7%) on the back of stronger-than-expected domestic demand in 1H24.

![](images/78749c3bd5330c5210544f6bd909485b8b0c83e14af64c18c60929bef8dd6819.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, TurkStat

We extend our output gap estimations until the end of 2025 by using our GDP growth forecasts. We estimate the output gap to remain positive at 1.8% in 1Q24 and 0.6% in 2Q24 (+1.2% in 1H24, Figure 4). We acknowledge that the output gap has fallen from its elevated levels in 2Q23, but the current level of economic activity still exerts upside pressure on inflation and the current account deficit as of 2Q24.

## ...but set to cool

We expect the output gap to turn negative from 3Q24 onwards as fiscal policy is likely to support monetary policy through public spending cuts. Our forecasts suggest a negative and widening output gap starting in 3Q24 (Table 1), based on the estimate of Türkiye\`s potential growth of 4.4% for 2024, and 4% for 2025. In other words, economic activity is likely to support disinflation over 2H24 and 2025.

![](images/636965a3ad69b8c2043227d977bbf74eb832c35f3f9ea71f5b802d451d6a453e.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, TurkStat

Table 1: J.P. Morgan estimations & forecasts
<table><tr><td>%</td><td>GDP Growth</td><td>Output Gap</td></tr><tr><td>2022</td><td>5.3</td><td>1.5</td></tr><tr><td>2023</td><td>4.5</td><td>1.4</td></tr><tr><td>2024</td><td>3.0</td><td>-0.1</td></tr><tr><td>2025</td><td>3.5</td><td>-0.9</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P. Morgan, TurkStat

## Output gap matters more for CA

While the output gap is one determinant of inflation, it also has important implications for the current account deficit. Figure 5 shows the quarterly current account balance with gray shaded areas showing periods of cooling in the economy with two or more consecutive negative output gaps. The current account turned to a surplus in 3Q18-2Q19 thanks to monetary tightening followed by severe currency weakness in August 2018. Current account adjustments were rather slow and incomplete following the rate hikes in 4Q20-3Q21 and 3Q23-1Q24 as the output gap remained positive (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Current account balance
![](images/114b6231d17f2f499a6f6480ee01756295eb61e5a16d3322dbd6b36616815361.jpg)

## Coordinated efforts to cool down activity

Post May 2023, authorities have delivered significant tightening in the form of rate hikes and macroprudential measures. The Turkish lira loan rates surged and commercial lira credit growth slowed, but credit card spending was not addressed. And fiscal policy was accommodative early in 2024 on expansionary wage policy and earthquake-related government spending. Also, FX loans accelerated in 1H24 and excess lira liquidity started to put downside pressure on lira deposit rates, both of which could be tackled with hikes in RRR.

We assess that the current level of the output gap requires further fiscal and liquidity measures to address the macro imbalances as the Turkish economy is still running above its potential. The first public savings package announced recently by Finance Minister Simsek is a small but right step to lower government spending for the next three years, in our view. We believe more is needed and authorities signalled further measures aimed to keep the government budget deficit below 5% of GDP this year. On wages, we believe no minimum wage hike is likely this year, in line with signals from President Erdogan.

In our view, authorities are delivering coordinated monetary and fiscal tightening. This mix is set to engineer and maintain a negative output gap for the first time since 1Q21, and help disinflation and the current account adjustment in 2H24 and 2025.

## United States

Home sales remain tepid to start the spring buying season

• Business investment spending still broadly trending sideways

The FOMC minutes indicate disappointment on inflation

• Next week<sup>’</sup>s April core PCE inflation reading expected up 2.8%

The most recent batch of economic indicators suggest the economy is expanding near a trend-like pace. Beneath the surface, however, the more interest-sensitive sectors look sluggish. April home sales continue to look listless, particularly in the resale market where little inventory is coming online. Meanwhile, the latest figures on capital goods orders and shipments indicate that business investment spending growth still looks subdued.

While business investment spending has been soft, business hiring has been buoyant. And the latest weekly figures on jobless claims point to a still-tight labor market. After a concerning bounce higher two weeks ago, new claims have since retreated to a low level of 215,000 in the most recent report. The employment signals from the nationwide PMI report haven<sup>’</sup>t been the most reliable indicators of job growth, but the latest reports suggest slower growth in service hiring though perhaps some pickup in factory employment.

The minutes from the early May FOMC meeting were released this week and confirmed that the Committee intends to maintain the current restrictive stance of policy longer than previously anticipated in response to the disappointing firstquarter inflation readings. There was also some reassessment of just how restrictive policy actually is, mirroring discussions occurring outside the Fed. And in contrast to Chair Powell<sup>’</sup>s more dovish press conference after the meeting, it appears that “various<sup>”</sup> Committee participants were more willing to tighten policy further should inflation risks appear to mount.

Among the data released next week will be the second look at 1Q GDP. The preliminary report printed at 1.6% annualized growth last quarter, and we look for that number to be revised lower to 1.1%. More closely watched will be the April core PCE inflation number, which we expect to be up 0.3% monthon-month (0.26% to two decimals) and 2.8%oya.

## Humdrum home sales

Home sales tanked in 2022 as the Fed began its aggressive tightening cycle. Since then housing demand has been stagnant at low levels. That continued in April, when existing home sales disappointed with a 1.9% decline to a 4.14mn pace (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Existing and pending home sales
![](images/f9dc041351ca1a51172e6d956ea86c6b4be2e81ab614d49c33e554731db02adb.jpg)

An annual sales pace around 4mn units is similar to where the market bottomed after the GFC and is likely the level consistent with the bare minimum turnover. New home sales don<sup>’</sup>t face the same constraint of reluctant sellers locked in by low mortgage rates. However, even with the greater availability of inventory (Figure 2), new home sales haven<sup>’</sup>t exactly been boomy. New sales were reported to have declined 4.7% on the month. While the monthly moves in this report can be quite noisy, the trend is still one of a restrained pace of sales growth as borrowing costs remain elevated.

Figure 2: Inventory of new single-family home sales
![](images/ccb91136128cbc15410c71bc2236faff8d285719e83da939f6998f4d6ce9a339.jpg)

## Cautious capex

The April durable goods report was a mixed bag in what it said about recent capital spending trends. Nominal shipments of core (nondefense, ex-aircraft) capital goods increased 0.4% last month, though the prior month was revised lower to show a 0.3% decline. A similar pattern also occurred for core capital goods orders. Stepping back, the level of such shipments in April was below the 1Q average (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Core capital goods orders and shipments \$billions, sa
![](images/4bea2e2c2cf1e3257b6ee446f20b9cca9a38be8c42a922a94a5683c2990f4d30.jpg)

The capital goods figures are an important input into estimating business spending on equipment in the GDP report. Consistent with the sideways pattern seen in Figure 3, the level of real equipment spending in 1Q of this year was almost identical to the level observed back in 2Q22.

## Comforting claims

Two weeks ago the weekly level of initial jobless claims jumped from 209,000 to 232,000. At the time we suspected that move reflected noisy seasonal adjustment, particularly for New York. Claims have slipped lower since then and the most recent reading printed at 215,000, near the springtime lows (Figure 4). Although some other indicators have pointed to a cooling in the demand for workers, the claims data are still suggesting a resilient labor market.

Figure 4: Initial claims in New York and other areas
![](images/0f0fa136517504606072ea93b709e00f3654f6f5ca9901c223bf034100f77551.jpg)

## May flash PMIs rebound

The May flash PMIs came in stronger, with the manufacturing composite rising 0.9 to 50.9 while services business activity rebounded 3.5pts to 54.8. These outcomes followed a few months of declines and meaningfully beat our and consensus expectations for a slight further softening in both. The details were supportive, with gains across nearly all survey components for both manufacturing and services. Accordingly, S&P Global reports that the all-industry composite jumped to a 25- month high.

Figure 5: US all industry output PMI and GDP growth
![](images/5d52a415efbf872ca452b102ab21b104fc50cc9889f298fed7102712a09bbad7.jpg)

To be sure, the PMIs haven<sup>’</sup>t been a great guide to predicting growth in this cycle (Figure 5). In fact, business surveys more generally haven<sup>’</sup>t been great guides. But the recovery in these figures may provide some reassurance that activity isn<sup>’</sup>t hitting an air pocket.

## Inflation expectations hold steady

Through the upside inflation surprise in 1Q, household inflation expectations have been relatively steady. In last week<sup>’</sup>s NY Fed Survey of Consumer Expectations, one-year ahead expected inflation moved up to 3.3% while three-year ahead moved down to 2.8%. In a similar pattern, in the final May reading of the University of Michigan survey, year-ahead inflation expectations ticked up to 3.3% while the five- to 10- year measure was unchanged at 3.0% and down a tick from the preliminary reading (Figure 6).

Figure 6: Michigan survey inflation expectations
![](images/df4aa3704e49c1faf855550843d313055558519046e43154090e9bb2b2ecd989.jpg)

The inflation story will remain front and center in next week<sup>’</sup>s holiday-shortened calendar, with the April core PCE number due up Friday. Anecdotal information from Wednesday<sup>’</sup>s Beige Book may also give some insight into how businesses see their cost structures and pricing power evolving.

We believe that real GDP expanded 1.1% saar in 1Q, below the BEA<sup>’</sup>s initial official estimate for last quarter of 1.6%. Most of this downward revision is due to incoming data over the past month that suggest slower consumer spending and less inventory accumulation. April real sales came in below expectations and shaved nearly 0.3%-pt from consumption, although the 1Q Quarterly Services Survey offered a small offset. All told, we think real consumer spending growth will be revised down modestly to 2.3%q/q, saar. Away from consumption, revisions to other parts of real domestic final sales are likely to be mixed and moderately reduce growth in this measure. We look for a slight downward revision to real equipment spending growth but a modest increase in real nonresidential structures (from a small drag in the initial release). We also expect slightly firmer government spending in the second release of 1Q GDP growth.

Trade and inventories continue to contribute to elevated volatility in the post-pandemic GDP statistics. The combination of the two contributed over a full percentage point of drag in the initial 1Q release, and we think that will be similar in the first revision, albeit with a slightly larger trade drag and a slightly smaller inventory drag than previously reported.

The upcoming GDP report will also contain the BEA<sup>’</sup>s first estimate of 1Q corporate profits. With continued solid growth of employee compensation and firming of interest costs to businesses, we look for corporate profits to have moved lower in 1Q, with a 3.2%q/q decline reported for the quarter.

We believe that the nominal goods trade balance widened to -\$92.0bn in April from -\$91.5bn in March, with exports declining 1.8% and imports falling 1.0%. These declines would extend the reductions in both exports and imports registered in March (in both value and volume terms). In light of the typical volatility seen in these data, we are looking for smaller declines in both components last month than in March. With exports expected to decline more than imports, we look for the deficit to widen further.

We look for the pending home sales index to slide 2.5% in April. If realized, this decline would follow two strong months of gains, and still leave the index above its level at the start of the year. Despite broad stabilization across a range of housing market data, a number of indicators have softened more recently, and we expect a similar move down in the pending home sale index.

<table><tr><td>Fri May 31 8:30am</td><td colspan="5">Personal income</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="10"></td><td>%m/m, sa, unless noted</td><td>Jan</td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td></tr><tr><td>Personal income</td><td>1.0</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Wages &amp; salaries</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.7</td><td>0.7</td><td>0.0</td></tr><tr><td>Consumption</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.8</td><td>0.8</td><td>0.6</td></tr><tr><td>Real consumption PCE price index</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>0.4</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td>Core</td><td>0.50 0.4</td><td>0.27</td><td>0.32</td><td>0.26</td></tr><tr><td>Mkt-Based Core</td><td>2.9</td><td>0.2 2.8</td><td>0.3 2.8</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Core (%oya) Mkt-Based Core (%oya)</td><td>2.9</td><td>2.7</td><td>2.6</td><td>2.8</td></tr><tr><td>Saving rate</td><td>4.1</td><td>3.6</td><td>3.2</td><td>2.8</td></tr></table>

The path forward for inflation remains a key focus for markets and policymakers, and with the April CPI and PPI data already in hand, we anticipate both the headline and core PCE price indexes will have advanced 0.3% in April (0.26% to two decimals for the core reading). Continued solid monthly gains imply that the year-ago inflation rate on headline should have held at 2.7% while core remained at 2.8% last month. We look for further disinflation as the year progresses but, as various Fed officials have noted, progress has been slower than previously expected. The core services excluding housing (“supercore<sup>”</sup>) price index appears likely to have risen 0.2% (0.24% unrounded) in April, with the year-ago inflation rate seen having cooled modestly to a still-elevated 3.4%oya last month.

Retail sales disappointed in April with a flat headline and a 0.3% drop in the oft-cited control measure that excludes gasoline, autos, building materials, and food. The former two categories posted solid nominal gains for April, and unit auto sales were strong as well. While the goods side of the consumption picture nonetheless looks to have softened some, we still look for relatively robust spending on services in April, continuing its elevated pace over the past several months as household fundamentals (including job growth and asset appreciation) remain supportive. Given the expected change in prices, we look for a 0.3% rise in real consumer spending for April.

The prior few months of income gains also have been strong, but we think those growth rates will have cooled in April. We look for nominal income to have increased 0.2% even as wage and salary growth was likely flat on the month. Thus we expect that real disposable income slipped 0.1% in April. Given our expectations for disposable income and spending, we think the saving rate slid 0.4%-pt to 2.8%, which if realized would mark the lowest reading since mid-2022.

<table><tr><td></td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td></td><td>Apr</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Total (mn, saar)</td><td>4.38</td><td>4.19</td><td>4.22</td><td>4.30</td><td>4.14</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m</td><td>9.5</td><td>-4.3</td><td>-3.7</td><td>2.6</td><td>-1.9</td></tr><tr><td>%oya nsa</td><td>0.7</td><td>-9.7</td><td>-9.5</td><td>19</td><td>6.8</td></tr><tr><td>Months&#x27; supply (nsa)</td><td>2.9</td><td>3.2</td><td></td><td></td><td>3.5</td></tr><tr><td>Single-family</td><td>2.8</td><td>3.1</td><td>3.0</td><td></td><td>3.4</td></tr><tr><td>Median price (%oya)</td><td>5.6</td><td>4.8</td><td>4.7</td><td></td><td>5.7</td></tr></table>

April existing home sales disappointed with a 1.9% decline, albeit from an upwardly-revised level for March. Nonetheless, sales slid to a 4.14mn saar pace last month after recently peaking in February at 4.38mn saar. Over the same period, existing sales of single-family homes fell 2.1% to a 3.74mn saar pace. These declines are at odds with upward moves in February and March in pending home sales, which typically lead existing home sales by a month or two. The April declines were broadly distributed geographically, and the tightness in this market eased marginally as months<sup>’</sup> supply rose to 3.5, which puts it back close to its level from late last year (after declining to 2.5 in February). With supply still tight, the median selling price rose 5.7% from a year ago. Stepping back, while various housing market data have softened somewhat in the past month or two, the broader trend appears to be one of stabilization after the large slowdown in 2022 as the Fed began its aggressive tightening cycle. Interest rates on 30-year mortgages have remained above 7% since the start of February.

The US May flash PMIs surprised stronger, with the manufacturing composite rising 0.9 to 50.9 while services business activity rebounded 3.5-pts to 54.8. These outcomes followed a few months of declines and meaningfully beat our and consensus expectations for a slight further softening in both. The details were supportive, with gains across nearly all survey components for both manufacturing and services. Accordingly, S&P Global reports that the all-industry composite jumped to a 25-month high. The momentum in this reading reinforces our expectation for US GDP growth to return to an abovetrend reading this quarter after a softer print for 1Q. (We are tracking a downward revision to 1Q growth that will put it modestly above 1.0% q/q saar from its initial 1.6%ar release, whereas we look for 2Q growth around 2.25%ar.)

In manufacturing, the output PMI jumped 1.3-pts to 52.4, while other key components also improved on the month (including a small decline in supplier deliver times, suggesting less potential supply chain dislocations). Our tracking estimate for the ISM-weighted composite recovered 1.0-pt to 50.7 for May, back above the 50-breakeven threshold. Future output also improved modestly in the May flash. That said, the ratio of new orders to inventories (the stocks of finished goods) declined modestly further below one in May, which points to less momentum for production relative to other aspects in this report.

In addition to the jump in the headline business activity measure, the flash services PMI report showed a partial recovery in the employment component to 49.4, but the level remains at its lowest point since the pandemic-induced recession (other than April). Yet the new business and future activity components of the services PMI improved and paint a more constructive picture of the services sector.

Turning to indicators of cost and price pressures for the May flash, input prices continue to climb for manufacturing but largely continue to trend sideways for services. Meanwhile, output prices were mixed across sectors but not significantly changed over the past several months.

<table><tr><td rowspan="2"></td><td rowspan="2">Feb</td><td rowspan="2"></td><td colspan="2">Mar</td><td rowspan="2">Apr 685</td><td rowspan="2">634</td></tr><tr><td>631</td><td>693</td></tr><tr><td>Total (000s,saar) %m/m</td><td>-637 -5.1</td><td>-5.0</td><td>8.8</td><td>665 5.4</td><td>12</td><td>-4.7</td></tr><tr><td>%oya nsa</td><td>1.8</td><td></td><td>8.1</td><td>3.2</td><td>09</td><td>-8.3</td></tr><tr><td>Months&#x27; supply</td><td>-8.8</td><td>8.9</td><td>-8.3</td><td>8.5</td><td></td><td>9.1</td></tr><tr><td>Median price (%oya)</td><td>-6.2</td><td>-3.6</td><td>-1.9</td><td>0.1</td><td></td><td>3.9</td></tr></table>

New home sales softened more than expected in April, declining 4.7% on the month and coming in at a meaningfully softer annualized pace of 634,000 for the month. This report largely revised away the strong bounce in March (now 665,000 versus 693,000 previously reported) and, outside of February, puts new home sales back down to a level last seen over a year ago. Geographically, sales declined in the Northeast, South and West, but improved in the Midwest. Months<sup>’</sup> supply also moved up while the median selling price rose 3.9%oya. Despite painting a more downbeat picture of the US housing market than the softening seen in some other recent data, historically this series is very volatile and prone to large revisions. As such, we don<sup>’</sup>t take a strong signal from this release.

<table><tr><td colspan="5">%m/m, sa</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>0.8</td><td>Apr 07</td></tr><tr><td>New orders Ex transportation</td><td>1.2</td><td>-0.9</td><td></td><td>0.7</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>0.4</td><td>0.0 -0.2</td><td>0.4 04</td><td>0.4 0.3</td></tr><tr><td>Nondef cap. gds ex air</td><td>0.5</td><td>-0.1 -0.0</td><td>ゆ</td><td>1.2</td></tr><tr><td>Shipments Nondef cap. gds ex air</td><td>1.0 -0.8</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.1 0.3</td><td>0.4</td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td>-0.3</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Inventories</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.0</td><td></td><td>0.1</td></tr></table>

New orders for durable goods surprised notably to the upside for April, with the headline rising 0.7% against our and consensus estimates for a decline of a roughly similar size. However, the March reading was revised down a tenth to 0.8%. (The initial reading last month was a much stronger 2.6% for March, but that was revised lower after the factory orders report.) Much of the upside surprise to the headline number was in defense, which jumped 13.2% on the month. Meanwhile, civilian aircraft orders fell -8% last month; a somewhat larger decline was anticipated. But the closely watched core capital goods orders were also firmer than expected, rising 0.3% in April. Overall, however, core capital goods orders have been trending flat to very slightly higher over the past year. Shipments of durable goods surprised to the upside as well, rising 1.2% in April. Both defense and non-defense aircraft shipments were also firm last month, while core capital goods shipments rose 0.4%—stronger than the flat reading we had expected, although again March was revised modestly lower. Inventory accumulation also appeared slightly stronger in the report, albeit with small downside revisions to March as well. All told, the revisions did not move 1Q tracking from 1.1% q/q saar, versus the initial release at 1.6%ar. While this report points to some slight upward risk to our 2Q GDP

growth forecast, it is still very early in the current quarter in terms of tracking the hard data.

<table><tr><td colspan="2">Consumer sentiment (May 24)</td><td colspan="2"></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Apr</td><td>Pre May</td><td>Fin May</td></tr><tr><td>Univ. of Mich. Index (nsa)</td><td>77.2</td><td>67.4</td><td>68.0 69.1</td></tr><tr><td>Current conditions</td><td>79.0</td><td>68.8</td><td>69.6</td></tr><tr><td>Expectations</td><td>76.0</td><td>66.5</td><td>68.8</td></tr><tr><td>Inflation expectations</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Short-term</td><td>3.2</td><td>3.5</td><td>3.3</td></tr><tr><td>Long term</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.1</td><td>3.0</td></tr></table>

The University of Michigan consumer sentiment index declined 8.1 points to 69.1 in the final May reading after several months of relatively flat prints. The preliminary report for May indicated a somewhat larger drop from April, which was partly reversed in the report. Both the current conditions and expectations components of the headline index had significant declines, down 9.4 and 7.2 from April, respectively. Even though rising stock prices and a fall in retail gasoline prices for the month to date likely supported consumer sentiment, it seems like the prospects of continued high interest rates and downbeat expectations about the labor market and income growth weighed on sentiment. One year-ahead inflation expectations edged up to 3.3% from 3.2% in April, but were down from the 3.5% reported in the preliminary reading earlier in the month.

Figure 1: Student loans 90+ days delinquent

## Focus: SHEDding light on student loan payments

Concern that an end to student loan forbearance would appreciably weaken consumer spending was widespread last year—but not apparent in the subsequent data. The Fed<sup>’</sup>s 2023 Survey of Household Economics and Decision-making (SHED) can help explain why. This release shows that 65% of student loan borrowers were required to start making payments as of October 2023, with 16% reported being behind on at least one student loan they owe—roughly matching prepandemic levels (66% and 17% in 2019, respectively). The combination of those not yet making payments and those missing payments is one likely factor that limited the impact of the end of forbearance last September. As missed federal student loan payments will not be reported to credit bureaus until 4Q24—precluding an anticipated jump in delinquencies (Figure 1)—the SHED represents the first bit of information we have about households restarting student loan payments.

![](images/0f97caae39fc99b59d0ad449a2990941063636ff33f86e6df228ddbc63beac0b.jpg)

Back in September, we observed a surge in deposits at the Department of Education, which we interpreted as a preemptive push by households to pay down some student loan debt. Payments have since moderated from this earlier peak (Figure 2), which is likely another factor cushioning the impact on household spending—much as we had anticipated.

Figure 2: Department of Education Receipts \$Bn, annualized monthly data
![](images/eaae98f750d45c27b16eece019e528d39c5d182e494ba942c93225bd81fa166c.jpg)

Another factor to support the relative strength in consumer spending despite the resumption of student loan debt payments involves the composition of debt across the income distribution. According to the Survey of Consumer Finances (SCF), even though more families in the bottom 40% of the income distribution had education loans in 2022 than in 2019 (the prior wave of the SCF), median balances declined for this cohort. In contrast, more families in the top 20% took up education loans and experienced significant increases in median balances over the same period, up from \$25,000 to \$50,000 (Figure 3). We can reconcile these facts if the resumption of loan repayment by higher-income households did not restrain their consumption, while lower-income households were challenged and started to miss payments. In fact, details from the SHED suggest that while 24% of families with income less than \$50,000 were behind on student loan payments, only about 7% of families with income more than \$100,000 were in the same situation.

Figure 3: Education loans - Survey of Consumer Finances (2019-2022) Change since 2019 survey by income percentile % pt change %c
![](images/7b4cc5ac96c3374c61ad8c00d8ccbe1c3b9c77388b749140fad893b25fb64eb4.jpg)

This difference in payment behavior very likely was reinforced by the composition of aggregate wealth accumulation since the pandemic. The bottom 20% of the income distribution clearly lagged whereas most households increased their liquid assets significantly since the end of 2019 (Figure 4). This extra liquidity might have helped higher-income households to sustain their consumption despite the apparent headwind from restarting student loan payments.

Figure 4: Increase in liquid assets by income group Change since 4Q19, both axes; solid bars are 4Q23, white are pandemic max
![](images/8afa57cc091ff39b665a12bb5cbcbeda9814af22529d1d8c213047cdbe68b7aa.jpg)
Source: FRB Distributional Financial Accounts, J.P. Morgan

## Euro Area

• Strong PMI prompted upward GDP revision

• 1Q wage data strong due to Germany

• 2026 ECB staff inflation forecast likely unchanged

The Euro area data this week showed that activity momentum strengthened through May. The rise in the PMI prompted us to revise our 2Q and 3Q GDP growth forecasts up to 1.5% ar. On the inflation front, wage inflation remained strong in 1Q, but this outcome was skewed toward Germany while wage inflation fell in other main economies of the region. The PMI survey also suggests that inflationary pressures have weakened in recent months. Finally, the cut-off date for the ECB staff forecast was this week and we expect this forecast to continue to show a return of core inflation towards 2% in 2026.

## GDP growth revised up

The Euro area composite output PMI continued to trend up in May (Figure 1). The survey increased 0.6pt to 52.3, while we and consensus expected a lower level (52.0). The strong momentum in the survey prompts us to raise our near-growth forecast. Based on our models, the survey signals that activity is running at a 1.5% ar pace. We thus raise our 2Q GDP forecast from 1.0% ar to 1.5% ar and assume a similar gain in 3Q (1.0% ar previously). But we leave our 4Q GDP forecast unchanged at 1.0% ar. One candidate for the strength in activity is the consumer. Households are benefitting from strong real wage gains that will remain firm through 3Q and should boost spending. But real wage gains should fade somewhat in 4Q. The manufacturing drag also looks to have faded significantly according to the PMI survey. IP details had hinted at some improvement among energy-intensive industries. With gas prices remaining low, energy-intensive output may increase further in the coming months and support activity in the near term.

Figure 1: Euro area real GDP
![](images/99b99e1c5c6f8cef7c110b50a7ebf89404fdd333bda70ab9f3ed00ef4bc06e09.jpg)

A key question is the impact of a stronger growth environment on consumer price inflation. We already highlighted upside risks around our core inflation forecast which sees core broadly flat around 2.6%oya for the rest of the year, before moving down towards 2% in 2H25. With growth running at an above potential pace in the near term, companies may retain more significant pricing power, while we (and the ECB) expect unit profit growth to soften. In addition, a strong outcome in Germany lifted Euro area negotiated pay growth in 1Q24 while productivity growth is still weak. However PMI price indices declined in May and the output price index overall stands in line with its 2017-2019 average, when core inflation stood significantly below 2%oya (Figure 2). And it looks as if the GDP deflator softened significantly in 1Q24 based on the national data. This highlights that developments are mixed even if risks are tilted to the upside.

Figure 2: Euro area PMI output prices
![](images/639549ed7d9dfbc19cf8bb921a08f56bbffc1586d1e9e1101d56539c0d5f692c.jpg)

## Mixed 1Q wage data in 1Q

The quarterly indicator of negotiated pay from the ECB came out at 4.7%oya (Figure 3). The data was stronger than expected, but much of the surprise was due to delayed pay deals and one-offs in Germany, while wage inflation in other countries has started to moderate.

Figure 3: Euro area wage growth
![](images/4f7ce8a9e19cd42dd3c7ad27b16a7abcc05933b3dcdcccbba0c30489244366dc.jpg)

Alongside the quarterly index, the ECB put out a staff blog on the issue, which reinforces the strong but also mixed message. In particular, the ECB has a Forward Wage Tracker, which looks at the forward implications of existing deals. For example, a deal may extend two years into the future and therefore the annual change can be exactly worked out if the timing of the various pay changes is known. Of course, this forward-looking tracker does not say anything about new deals that are being struck.

Considering the latest data, the ECB noted that the forward implications of deals have been moderating. At the time of the December 2023 policy meeting, the staff saw existing deals giving a 4.7%oya increase for 2024. By the time of the April meeting, this had declined to 4.1%oya. This could be consistent with developments outside of Germany, where the official negotiated pay indices have slowed in 1Q24, possibly by more than expected. The upside surprise from Germany in 1Q24 could reverse some of the shift in the orange line, but the ECB also mentioned that German deals are still responding to the high inflation of the past. For example, the ECB calculates that by March 2024, only 75% of German workers had received the tax-exempt inflation compensation payments.

## ECB forecast to keep disinflation in place

Eurosystem staff forecasts are going to be a key input into the ECB<sup>’</sup>s next meeting on 6 June. Back in March, a relatively modest downward revision of the 2H24 inflation forecast of 0.1%-pt on headline and 0.15%-pt on core inflation had an apparently large impact on discussions in the Governing Council, contributing to the strong signal for a June cut. With that cut seemingly a done deal, the new forecasts could have a big impact on what the ECB signals for subsequent meetings.

The new forecasts are likely being finalized by the middle of this week and are likely using technical assumptions with a cut-off date that was at the start of last week. In terms of the technical assumptions, interest rates will be higher, more so on money market rates (50bp-ish) than on bond yields. In contrast, the currency is barely changed: up 0.8% in tradeweighted terms and 0.4% down against the dollar. Oil prices are likely up 5% across the forward curve, which however has the same downward slope as before. Gas and electricity prices are barely changed, with gas at a relatively low €30/ MWh over the forecast horizon.

On growth, incoming data have been on the firm side. 1Q24 GDP printed 1.3%q/q saar, above the staff<sup>’</sup>s 0.3%q/q saar forecast. And surveys are running above the staff<sup>’</sup>s 1%q/q saar for 2Q24. Further out, the staff will likely feel comfortable with the 1.6% pace it has in the forecast. The higher oil price and higher rates may only take a bit out of that for late 2025 and into 2026. But an improving global backdrop and a solid real income constellation for households are likely still two key drivers of this relatively firm growth outlook.

In terms of the inflation narrative, compensation per employee was broadly in line in 4Q24 and negotiated pay data have been broadly stable. The 4.5% annualized run-rate that the staff expected for compensation per employee for 2024 still looks reasonable, with pronounced slowing expected only next year. We suspect the staff will feel comfortable with this profile, even if unemployment is showing no signs of increasing (as it had expected) and even if per employee compensation in 1Q24 could be a tick firmer than expected.

The bigger challenge is that employment is not stalling in 1H24 as the staff had expected, which weighs on productivity and puts upward pressure on unit labour costs. But, we suspect the staff will still expect a solid pickup in productivity, albeit a delayed one, which implies that unit labour cost growth will still be expected to slow significantly over time. In addition, the near-term implications of the delayed productivity pickup is dampened by unit profits, which seems to be limiting the likely miss on the GDP deflator in 1Q24. Overall, this leaves the broad inflation narrative in place, even if there are some pressure points.

Turning to consumer prices themselves, the main challenge is the starting point, with core inflation in 1Q24 possibly 0.2%-pt higher than the 2.5%oya expected by the staff in March (Table 1). This may not seem like a big gap, but reflects stickiness in services inflation. Higher oil prices may also add a bit of upward pressure, while a higher interest rate assumption may offset some of this. Overall, we can see core inflation revised up 0.1%-pt for 2025 to 2.2%oya, which would still show 2H25 at 2%oya, with an increase only at the second decimal. In some sense, this forecast will be based on fewer rate cuts than in March.

As a final point, the June projections involve the entire Eurosystem, whereas the March update was done by the ECB staff. There has been a sense that the Eurosystem projections have been more hawkish than the ECB projections, but there is no clear sign of this from national central bank presidents. Hence, June appears on track, while the sense of caution in some commentary for the period beyond June will evolve with the data over the coming months.

The PMI signaled a stronger pace of activity through May and the future output component (a measure of sentiment) increased in May. Therefore, we penciled in a rise in the EC economic sentiment index. This would keep the signals of the PMI and EC survey broadly aligned. The details of the EC survey will be important to monitor. In particular, it will be interesting to see whether pricing intentions have continued to decline in May.

The German composite PMI rose very strongly in May, with gains in services and manufacturing. Manufacturing was still lower in level terms, but has also improved significantly. While the linkage between the PMI and IFO has been very imperfect over the past two years, we do expect the IFO to also improve in May. It is also starting from a lower level and therefore has scope to not only match the PMI<sup>’</sup>s gain in May but to also catch up.

## Demand and labor markets

<table><tr><td colspan="2"></td><td>Jan</td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td></tr><tr><td>Thu</td><td>Euro area</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>May 30</td><td>Unemployment rate (%, sa)</td><td>6.5</td><td>6.5</td><td>6.5</td><td>6.5</td></tr><tr><td>11:00am</td><td>Unemployment (ch m/m, 000s)</td><td>21.0</td><td>22.0</td><td>-94.0</td><td></td></tr></table>

We expect the Euro area unemployment rate to remain stable at 6.5% in April, a historical low. The pace of activity in the region has increased looking at the signal of the PMI and the survey suggests that employment growth has been close to 1.3% ar so far in 2Q. The detailed 1Q national account data in early June will be important to monitor. Available information so far suggests that productivity likely was stable at the start of 1Q as GDP growth was matched by a similar gain in employment growth.

<table><tr><td></td><td>Jan</td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td></tr><tr><td>Fri Germany</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>May 31</td><td>Sales ex. autos and petroleum, volumes, sa</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>8:00am %m/m</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-1.5</td><td>2.3</td><td>0.0</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>-1.1</td><td>-2.2</td><td>0.7</td><td></td></tr></table>

German retail sales rose strongly in March, more than reversing February<sup>’</sup>s decline. In our view, real disposable incomes are only now turning higher in Germany and therefore an underlying improvement should also become apparent in retail spending. The data are however noisy and therefore this is always hard to predict in individual releases. We have pencilled in a cautious forecast that consolidates the increase in March.

The April core price gain was softer than in previous releases but this largely owes to weak core goods prices, while services prices were sticky. We expect Euro area core prices to be up 0.2%m/m as well in May, with a similar mix. This would leave the core year-on-year inflation rate stable at 2.7%oya, in contrast to the ECB which has a 2.5%oya core forecast for 2Q.

Consumer confidence continued to increase in May. The level of the survey still remains below its long term trend but the recent improvement in our view should continue to support household spending. The details of the survey are not out yet. But it will be interesting to see whether that increase was driven by better sentiment about personal finances, as was the case in recent months. The details will be published next week.

The final report left 1Q24 real GDP unrevised at 0.9%q/q saar. The details were weak in terms of domestic demand, with declines in consumer spending, government consumption and most of capex. The only support was a big jump in construction spending, which was helped by mild weather. In fact, the weakness in non-construction capex was even more pronounced when the relatively large gains in government capex are excluded. As a result, this week<sup>’</sup>s report is weak from a domestic-demand perspective. The support instead came from net trade, reflecting gains in exports and imports following a prolonged period over which both had declined. While all of this leaves the 1Q24 GDP gain looking underwhelming, business surveys have picked up strongly into 2Q24, which points to a broader improvement ahead.

Euro area negotiated pay accelerated to 4.7%oya in 1Q24, matching last summer<sup>’</sup>s peak. This reflected a strong pickup in Germany, whereas other countries moderated. In our view, the German pickup reflects delayed one-off payments and

delayed pay settlements, some of which are only now responding to the high inflation of the past. As a result, we think that the moderation in other countries contains the more meaningful signal. There is still a long way to go before wage growth becomes consistent again with the inflation target, but we would view the 1Q24 outcome as being an encouraging first step in that direction.

## Japan

Business surveys sent mixed signals, but signal a manufacturing recovery ahead

This echoed a jump in core machinery orders, pointing to a rise in capex

• April CPI moderated, with a decline in food inflation, but underlying momentum remained firm

We expect the scheduled rise in electricity prices supports further changes in firms’ price-setting behavior

This week<sup>’</sup>s data sent encouraging signals, indicating that production activity is heading for recovery from the sharp plunge at the start of the year. In the May flash PMI report, the manufacturing index surpassed 50 for the first time in a year, driven by a strong improvement in the output index, as auto plant reopening and supply chain normalization are driving a broader production recovery. The production recovery, coupled with strong corporate earnings, is beginning to ripple into capex and trade recovery. This week<sup>’</sup>s data supported our expectation of a growth recovery in 2Q, largely driven by strong corporate activity.

The April nationwide CPI continued to show a downward trend in core (ex. fresh food) inflation, the de-facto headline measure, on the back of easing cost pressures, particularly for foods. But underlying price momentum remained strong, with core (ex. food and energy) inflation rising strongly at 0.3%m/ m, sa, reflecting price revisions at the beginning of the fiscal year. We expect cost pressures to reemerge starting from May, as electricity prices are expected to rise sharply with a rise in the renewable energy charge and the removal of the subsidy. We expect this combined with the recovery of manufacturing activity will drive further changes in corporate pricing behavior, leading inflation to remain well above the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s target throughout the year.

## Signals of production recovery

The May all-industry flash PMI ticked up 0.1pt 52.4, with a mixed combination of a strong bounce in the manufacturing PMI and a moderation of the services PMI from an elevated level (Figure 1). The manufacturing PMI rose 0.9pt to 50.5, exceeding 50 for the first time in a year. A jump in the output index from a depressed level drove the strong recovery. Production activity had been depressed since the start of the year due to the Noto earthquake and auto factory shutdowns, but the May flash PMI report pointed to an upcoming recovery. The services activity PMI fell 0.7pt to 53.6, partly reversing the gains of the prior months. However, the index remained elevated, with limited signs of weakening despite rising concerns about domestic demand.

The May PMI also sent some signs of inflation moving higher in coming months. The manufacturing output price index rose 0.1pt to 55.7 after April<sup>’</sup>s 2.1pt jump, and the services output prices PMI stayed at 55.12, close to the decade high, even after a modest 0.4pt fall in April. Although CPI inflation has moderated lately, mainly due to the base effects in goods inflation, the PMI is indicating the upcoming reemergence of price pressures.

Figure 1: PMI output Index
![](images/368cd84da4c4dc8bc1d62c42165faab5140a053a271bcf699036ec62be7060f6.jpg)

On the other hand, the May Reuters Tankan survey sent mixed messages. Sentiment among large manufacturers disappointed a bit, moving sideways and tracking 9pts, although it is still only a few points below its peak this cycle. Despite some improvement in material sectors, sentiment dropped among electrical machinery and autos/transport equipment sectors. Although this raised some concerns on the near-term outlook, manufacturers also look for recovery ahead, with the outlook index pointing to a 2pt improvement to 11. Services sector sentiment remains elevated. The 10pt rise in information/communication sector sentiment and transportation sector sentiment offset the retail sector<sup>’</sup>s decline from its overtwo-decade-high level.

With business sentiment sending some signs of production recovery, this week<sup>’</sup>s machinery orders sent a encouraging signs on manufacturers<sup>’</sup> capex. March core private machinery orders surprisingly rose 2.9%m/m, sa, after the solid 7.7% increase in February. This brings its 1Q24 average up to 18.7%q/q, saar (Figure 2). The unexpected March rise was driven by manufacturers such as those of electrical machinery, automobiles, and general machinery. Orders from electrical machinery increased over 10% after rising more than 30% in February, to their highest level since October 2022.

Figure 2: GDP-based capex and core private machinery orders
![](images/e9d0428b6bf600e146abc2b6a2a41e270bf11f9bcf7ec111490ac2661c69795e.jpg)
Source: CAO, J.P. Morgan. Shaded area is J.P. Morgan forecast.

## Exports tread water in April

Japan<sup>’</sup>s April export volumes rose 0.1%m/m, sa, broadly in line with our expectations. The flat outcome last month left exports down 6.8%3m/3m, saar, though the pace of contraction is beginning to moderate after an over-10% fall in March (Figure 3). We think external demand should recover moderately through 2Q, in line with easing supply-chain disruption that has negatively affected shipments of autos and parts.

Against this backdrop, auto export volumes were up a strong 8.0%m/m, sa, offsetting declines in exports of tech (-1.9%) and capital goods (-2.1%). By destination, real exports to the EU and US were each up around 2%m/m, sa, though exports to other destinations were mixed. Tech export volumes to the rest of the Asia region slipped moderately, but in level terms remain relatively high, suggesting that a regional tech cycle upswing is providing some support to Japan<sup>’</sup>s electronics shipments.

Figure 3: Japan real exports and imports %3m/3m saar
![](images/8d0db3ccfd503a958cc0c63e6c903c2833d6687096f387685822cd9ed4a50739.jpg)

## Inflation silence before the storm

The April nationwide CPI showed a trend moderation in inflation pressures, while underlying inflation still remained solid. The global standard measure of core prices (ex. food and energy) rose 0.3%m/m, sa, leaving oya inflation at 2.0%. This was largely driven by core goods prices, which rose 0.4%m/m, sa, while services fell 0.1%. The drop in services inflation was partly attributable to the policy change in the Tokyo Metropolitan Office that removed household income thresholds for free high school education (Figure 4). Details in services inflation sent mixed signals, with strong pressures from culture and recreation (6.2%oya) and private transportation (3.4%) contrasting with persistent low inflation in housing (0.6%) and miscellaneous (1.2%). Outside of core, food inflation moderated 0.1%m/m, sa, but energy inflation jumped 0.8%. This left core (ex. fresh food) inflation, the defacto headline measure, moving sideways.

Figure 4: nationwide CPI, services
![](images/61790c48e7a7bb70b67a2af00ef1704af1924a0f4c8830ae448e53581fb2a761.jpg)

We think the recent moderation in inflation will be transitory. Energy inflation is expected to rise sharply starting from May, due to a rise in the renewable energy charge and the scheduled removal of the subsidy. This, together with renewed cost pressures from the weaker yen and higher nominal wages, likely will drive further changes in firms<sup>’</sup> price-setting behavior. We expect this to push up underlying inflation starting from the summer, leaving Japan<sup>’</sup>s inflation sticky (Figure 5). We think this will support the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s policy normalization.

Figure 5: Core CPI inflation forecast
![](images/a1fb338b28bba677bd0c3f86ba8b4d4ffeca98f8358923a7c643ac726ed8341a.jpg)

Week of May 27 - 31

Wed Consumer sentiment

<table><tr><td>May 29 2:00pm</td><td>Dl, sa</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="6"></td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>Consumer sentiment</td><td>39.1</td><td>39.5</td><td>38.3</td></tr><tr><td>Standard of living</td><td>37.6</td><td>37.5 36.1</td><td>38.0</td></tr><tr><td>Income growth</td><td>40.8</td><td>41.5</td><td>41.1</td></tr><tr><td>Labor market conditions</td><td>44.3</td><td>45.0</td><td>44.2</td></tr><tr><td>Durable goods purchases</td><td>33.5</td><td>34.0</td><td>31.8</td></tr></table>

1. The DI asks whether a respondent thinks that now is a good time to purchase durables

We expect consumer sentiment to fall in May, reflecting the recent rising concerns about higher inflation with the weakening of the yen and the scheduled removal of the energy subsidy.

## Canada

Headline and core CPI decelerated in April as we expected

• We are sticking with our call that the Bank of Canada will cut the policy rate on June 5

• We revise up our 1Q24 real consumption forecast from 2.5% to 3.0%ar

The April CPI report came in pretty much as we expected. Headline CPI decelerated to 2.7%oya and eased to 0.2% on a seasonally adjusted monthly basis in April. This week<sup>’</sup>s report is the last the Bank of Canada will see before the June 5 monetary policy announcement. All three of the Bank of Canada<sup>’</sup>s core measures were in the target range; the 3-month trend pace of the Bank<sup>’</sup>s cores is the lowest in over three years (Figure 1). With the further easing in underlying inflation sustained, we expect the Bank of Canada will be comfortable taking back some of its policy restraint next month. The Bank has stressed that it was looking for “further and sustained easing in core inflation.<sup>”</sup> We look for the BoC to deliver a first rate cut at its June 5 meeting. We also continue to expect the BoC will cut its policy rate 150bp by mid-2025.

Figure 1: Bank of Canada core CPIs
![](images/89a3d9df66c16aa74b6880956c7e0f495f61041c94c7c73c31999a301c2082a3.jpg)

Gasoline prices jumped in April but the news away from higher prices at the pump was favorable as there was broadbased deceleration in the pace of inflation in food prices (2.3%oya), services prices (4.2%oya in April compared with 4.5%oya in March), and durable goods prices (-0.8%oya). Excluding gasoline, the all-items CPI slowed to 2.5%oya. Note that gasoline prices are tracking close to a 1.0%m/m, nsa decline in May. The core CPI (excluding food and energy) also posted a 2.7% annual change as we expected. On a seasonally adjusted basis the core was up just 0.1%m/m. The breadth of above-target price pressures, a metric the Bank of Canada has been monitoring closely, continued to trend lower in April (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Breadth of elevated inflation share of CPI components over 3.0%oya, monthly data
![](images/09758746640f5672f47324110b1f7b39e4cf33625a8ab13d1ab6b2c5e5c74fd4.jpg)

## Window shopping

Retail sales decreased 0.2% in March, weaker than expected, while volume retail sales decreased 0.4%, a second consecutive decline. Despite the disappointing results in March, impressive momentum early in the quarter points to solid growth in real goods consumption in 1Q24, similar to the growth in 4Q23. And real services consumption as suggested by the tracking estimate of real GDP services output, which skews toward household demand, also suggests a robust gain last quarter. We revise up our 1Q24 real consumption forecast from 2.5%ar to 3.0%ar. Our latest estimate on the tracking drag from 1Q24 net trade offsets the upward revision so we hold our 1Q24 real GDP growth forecast at 2.0%ar.

In the other closely watched retail sales metric, core retail sales—which exclude gasoline station and auto dealer sales— were down 0.6% in March. Weakness in March was broadbased with building materials & supplies dealers the only core retail subcategory to report an increase. Retail sales overall related to residential demand were about flat. And away from the core, the largest increase in retail sales was at auto & parts dealers (+1.2%). The April flash is very favorable; the early read suggests that retail sales increased 0.7% last month. With the pace of inflation easing over the past year, long-term growth in real retail sales and nominal sales has re-aligned. And the trend has generally been downward over the same time period (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Retail sales, longer term trends %3m/12m, saar
![](images/74d5613eada3b75bc4bc1f0c13235b6e4e801b98002b79dcc36d9390f2755786.jpg)
