## Mexico

• In the minutes gradualism dominates the discussion but a June cut still on the table

• Core CPI displaying a more favorable trend, even as goods bounced

• 1Q GDP at 1.1%ar sets 2Q on a strong path, but risks of cooling in 2H persist

• TB - Domestic demand is strong; solid manufacturing

This week the central bank of Mexico published the minutes related to the May policy decision in which the monetary policy committee (COPOM in Spanish) paused the easing cycle at 11%. Overall, the key message from the board was the need to remain cautious and vigilant given the balance of risks to inflation (to the upside), but it also underscored the importance of carefully analyzing incoming data in each decision in order to take one decision at a time.

Still, the majority of members emphasized the current gains in the disinflation process, and once again anchored the narrative on the extremely high level of the real ex-ante policy rate and how this is not congruent with the inflation gap and the neutral policy rate (Figure 1). In our view, this approach is congruent with gradual (25bp) and intermittent cuts ahead.

Figure 1: Ex-ante policy and neutral real rate (r\*)
![](images/f1f6d3d8cc41b3fb68f0789628301c327a9f0a3912776da1f82775948e1e0224.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan and Banxico

“Hawkish<sup>”</sup> references were a common theme again in the policy discussion, even for those neutral/dovish members, but the bulk of these comments probably came from the usual suspect that dissented in March, while the member that has been advocating for fine-tuning responses suggested that he won<sup>’</sup>t endorse a cut in June and gave more hints about the importance of the Fed factor and the need to await further gains in the disinflation process before re-engaging in cuts.

For both of these members, inflation expectations remain paramount, even if in the past these have not declined persistently beyond the current levels (around 3.5%), an issue that probably will gain importance as the Bank further reduces its tight stance.

Growth concerns were mentioned across the board and while the COPOM<sup>’</sup>s approach was consistent with our own view (a soft patch followed by a 2Q24 bounce) it was the first time in a while that the Bank engaged in significant discussion about economic activity given below-par growth in 4Q23 (revised down this week to 0.0%saar) and a modest recovery in 1Q24 (1.1%saar).

Two final words of caution. First, board members are still aware of the challenges within core services inflation and the fact that the staff had to upwardly revise its inflation path (Figure 2) along with further delays in the convergence to the 3% inflation target. In fact, we still expect inflation to surpass Banxico<sup>’</sup>s forecast for 2Q24, which could complicate the board<sup>’</sup>s decision next month. Still, this week<sup>’</sup>s encouraging news on inflation (see next section for further details) is in line with expectations of further improvements in core services, which should allow the central bank to accommodate intermittent cuts ahead.

Figure 2: Inflation deviations from long-term levels for May
![](images/e51accb8fa809cbf6f922ec43bc071443a0edd908f130a0a851da9b5450a207c.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan with data from INEGI and IMSS. Data for May 2024 is forecast

We still believe Banxico will cut 25bp in June mostly on the grounds of a very restrictive monetary policy stance, but for this, inflation needs to continue to deliver welcome news in 2H May, and early June. A 3-2 vote in favor of a cut seems likely next month. All eyes will be on Banxico<sup>’</sup>s QIR report next week.

## A welcome surprise in services inflation

The headline and core CPI data for 1H May came in very much in line with expectations, with prices dropping 0.21% and rising 0.15%, respectively. The decline in prices owes to the usual seasonal suspect: electricity subsidies as we move into the summer. Annual inflation rates rose a tenth for the headline (4.8%) and stayed flat for the core (4.3%).

But that aside, the relevant bits and pieces were in the core CPI composition. Core prices increased a touch less than we expected (0.18%), but the composition was very different from past releases (dozens of them). Core goods were a bit firmer and our forecast for the month stands at 0.3%m/m, sa. Looking at %3m, saar rates, nonfood goods do not appear to

Source: INEGI

Figure 4: GDP: Services

be as big a disinflationary force as before, as we expect prices to have been up 2.5% (including our May forecast) from a low of 1.6% last December, but we are not concerned at this point as we consider this to be part of the natural mean-reversion process.

On the flip side, services inflation is certainly still running hot, with services and services ex. housing and education (“other services<sup>”</sup>) set to rise a sizable 0.4%m/m, sa and 0.6%, respectively. However, a good part of the increase was explained by the volatile airfares category, whereas the more underlying trend in non-tourism services surprised in a big way to the downside.

We were looking for a roughly 0.5%-0.6%m/m, sa gain in this category prior to this week<sup>’</sup>s release; after it, we are looking at 0.3%m/m, in line with its norm (Figure 3). This follows a year-to-date average increase of 0.6%. As said, it is only one print and non-tourism is still running at 6.7%ar, nevertheless doves are likely to be encouraged by the print.

Figure 3: Core CPI - Non-tourism services
![](images/fbbb80ae2356fa56c1aad115564a6e45b572e84726701f5ef1379836eb98b410.jpg)
Source: INEGI and J.P. Morgan. May data is J.P. Morgan's forecast

## GDP data signal slowdown was transitory

1Q GDP was revised up 0.8%ar to 1.1%. Earlier data were subject to several revisions, including: 2023 GDP growth reported at 3.2%y/y vs. 3.1% before, even as 2H growth was revised lower, whereas monthly growth was revised a tenth higher for Dec, Jan, and Feb. The overall picture shows us that activity slowed to a halt at the turn of the year, but bounced up strongly afterwards, particularly services activity (Figure 4).

GDP growth printed at 0% in 4Q, but February and March GDP growth was on average 0.9%m/m, sa. This was not sufficient to meaningfully lift 1Q GDP, which as said was up just 1.1%ar. But it did set a strong trajectory going into the current quarter, with statistical carryover alone (i.e., if we assume flat Apr-June GDP) of 3%-pts. We still look for marked cooling in 2H on the election hangover, but things are looking good for our forecast for a pop in growth in 2Q (4.2%ar).

Adding credence to our view of a strong pickup in GDP this quarter, this week<sup>’</sup>s April trade data were strong growth-wise. Domestic demand indicators were robust with consumer and capital imports up roughly 28%3m/3m, saar and 23%, respectively. Intermediate goods imports strength, meanwhile, pointed to sustained gains in factory output/exports ahead.

![](images/a032f2b73ba6d4d25b9e93096dbf6be583a435cc859ab3f18edf45972eb90350.jpg)

## Data releases and forecasts

## Brazil

• A less eventful week, for a change

• Government<sup>’</sup>s bimonthly fiscal reviews continue to underestimate expenditures and overestimate revenues

• Lower House to discuss VAT regulation until July

• We revised our 2024 CAD forecast up two-tenths to 1.6% of GDP

Following several weeks in which noise over economic policymaking led to turbulence in Brazil<sup>’</sup>s markets, this week was quieter in terms of both data and announcements. The release of the second bimonthly fiscal review updated the official estimate for this year<sup>’</sup>s primary deficit to BRL14.5bn (0.1% of GDP)—below the target for a balanced budget, but still within the tolerance band of up to a BRL28bn deficit (0.25% of GDP). The estimate, which does not include the projected BRL13.0bn in extraordinary transfers due to Rio Grande do Sul floods, accounts for increases of BRL6.3bn and BRL24.4bn in this year<sup>’</sup>s net revenues and expenditures (Table 1).

Table 1: Evolution of official fiscal forecasts for 202

Source: Finance Ministry, Congress and J.P. Morgan

Much like previous estimates, the updated review continues to include some fairly optimistic assumptions for revenues and underestimates expenditures. The main difference from March was on oil-related revenues, as the government increased royalties and, to a larger extent, dividends estimate. While expected revenues for social security improved, likely reflecting the better outlook for the labor market, it was more than offset by the increase in its outlays, the underestimation of which will likely require a reshuffle in due time.

The next review will be released in July, by which time another upward revision to pension and benefits payments could be enacted. It will also allow for a better glimpse of tax collection, which continued to grow quickly in April according to the IRS and may be reinforced by some non-recurrent events in the near term, such as the payment of deferred taxes on offshore investments (Figure 1). The July review will also be the last before the 2025 budget is delivered in late August. Revenue growth will be the key parameter to weigh whether the government will stand by the balanced fiscal target for 2024. But, looking further into the future, we deem spending pressures as equally, if not more, relevant, as they may dictate whether the political establishment will stand by the current framework or propose changes to it over the coming year.

## VAT regulation to move forward in LH

Speaker Arthur Lira created two working groups in the Lower House, with seven members each, to discuss the two main proposals to regulate the new VAT. While one will be dedicated to the proposal unveiled a month ago, another will discuss the bill on the federative aspects of the new system that Finance Minister Fernando Haddad promised for next week (“2º projeto complementar da tributária sai até 29 de maio, diz Haddad,<sup>”</sup> Poder360, May 21, 2024).

The groups will have 60 days to deliver their assessment, taking into consideration not only the bills sent by the government but also the views of other stakeholders. If they use the full 60 days, this would push meaningful votes to August, as the midyear recess is scheduled to start on July 18. A swifter analysis may still happen, in which case we would see a higher probability of having it approved in both houses of Congress before year-end.

## Revising CAD forecast up to 1.6% of GDP

In line with recent revisions to our fiscal and inflation forecasts, we are assuming some losses of the floods in Rio Grande do Sul state will impact Brazil<sup>’</sup>s net exports. Even if the extent of the damage remains unclear, and some reports suggest most of the crops were already harvested, we are revising up our current account deficit (CAD) forecast twotenths to 1.6% of GDP (Table 2).

That said, the April current account deficit was at US\$ 2.5 billion, wider than our forecast of US\$ 1.8 billion and consensus of US\$ 1.4 billion. While the surprise was concentrated in profits and dividends sent abroad, it remains striking the crypto net imports reached all-time highs and widened to 0.7% of GDP. CAD widened to 1.6% of GDP, rising from 1.2% at the beginning of the year, but is still at historically low levels especially if crypto imports are excluded (Figure 2). Small value imports stand at 0.3% of GDP.

Figure 2: Current account balance
![](images/3e3ae09ff3699f065f510b36d30f0b2f651af51a11f42abbe646690010533e8e.jpg)

Foreign direct investment (FDI) was reported at US\$ 3.9 billion, below the consensus of US\$ 5.0 billion and our aboveconsensus forecast of US\$ 5.5 billion, but still above the current account deficit. FDI continues to run relatively high— financing 2x the CAD—at 3% of GDP as direct equity investments, reinvestments, and intercompany loans seem to have stabilized around 1.4% of GDP, 1.1%, and 0.5%, respectively.

Domestic portfolio flows were also quite weak, registering outflows of US\$ 6.7 billion, mostly coming out of local fixed income that saw the worst result in more than two years, with small outflows for equity and equally small inflows for external debt. In the last 12 months, Brazil is registering portfolio outflows of about 0.1% of GDP, a result of local market debt inflows falling from 0.5%-0.6% in the last months to only 0.3% of GDP and equity outflows reaching 0.4% of GDP, the largest number registered since the pandemic.

## Argentina

Real activity contracted again in March, as expected, down by 1.4%m/m, sa

• Ahead, we expect activity to stabilize in 2Q23, and resume growth later in the year

The Treasury reported a new primary surplus in April equivalent to US\$0.3bn, the fourth in a row

The headline balance posted a 0.2% of GDP surplus YTD, the largest seen for such period since 2008

• As spending contraction starts to ease, securing additional revenues becomes paramount

## The seventh activity contraction in a row

Real activity contracted again in March, as expected, against the substantial compression of real purchasing power and fiscal adjustment. The monthly activity indicator fell by 1.4%m/ m, when adjusting for seasonality and calendar effects. That fall was a bit larger than what we had expected, though January and February were revised a tad higher. All in, the recession environment has not let up, as activity has contracted every month since September last year.

The 3-month sequential growth converged to -11.5% ar (our forecast was for -13%), and the activity level moved to 6.1%, sa below the cyclical peak in September 2023. On an overyear-ago basis, economic activity contracted by 8.4%oya in March, but when controlling for calendar effects the plunge comes closer to 6.1%oya, roughly in line with our forecast, and the largest contraction since the pandemic. Following the March report we fine-tune our projection for 1Q24 to a 12%ar contraction.

In the second quarter of the year, the normalization of agriculture production should start aiding headline activity more visibly. Therefore, our focus will be on ex. agriculture activity, which plummeted by 14.4% ar in the first quarter of the year in our estimates (Figure 2). The decoupling of headline and ex. agricultural activity will be magnified as the recovery of agri conditions should boost economic activity, largely masking the over-year-ago contraction of ex. primary production.

Compared with prior activity collapses, the recession so far has been fairly similar to the 2018 sudden stop, though the performance through March suggests that the overall drop may prove shallower. However, we note that the economic activity level is below those seen in 2018, thus a lower starting point implies that reaching pre-2018 sudden stop levels is more challenging (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Comparing the current recession to prior events Real activity, index , sa, (t=0) pre recession level
![](images/6d7ce501f55bb2deb6e2a62165831ddfd66d6e2fca9c25e1c7b3d43b9b251ec3.jpg)

Ahead, we expect activity to stabilize on a seasonally adjusted basis in 2Q23, and resume growth later in the year. As we discussed in a recent note, having activity growth reignite in the second half the year appears to be key to seeing the fruits of the early stabilization effort. On this front, we don<sup>’</sup>t expect either fiscal or monetary policies to become expansive—if anything just less restrictive in the second half of the year. Disinflation should help with the stabilization and the improvement of real purchasing power. But we argue the key to unleashing higher sustainable growth may be a credibility shock. In our view, such a shock could be associated with the removal of the web of capital controls. That shock, together with regulation and legal changes, some of them already in effect by executive order, should help private sector investment to fuel a recovery from its lows (see note for discussion).

## Spending contraction starts to ease

The Treasury reported a new primary surplus in April equivalent to US\$0.3bn, the fourth in a row. The fiscal consolidation thus remains on track, with the YTD primary surplus reaching 0.7% of GDP, compared to the 0.9% of GDP shortfall reported in the first four months of 2023. That said, as expected, the primary fiscal surplus has narrowed when compared to prior months<sup>’</sup> results, as the spending contraction has started to ease and the revenues pothole lingers (see below for more details).

At the time, the headline balance (including interest payments) posted a 0.2% of GDP surplus YTD, in line with prior month and the largest seen for such period since 2008. This represents a sizeable fiscal consolidation when compared to a 1.5% of GDP shortfall in same period of 2023. On a last 12- month basis, the headline balance is running at a 1.4% of GDP deficit, compared to a 4.4% shortfall by year-end.

The YTD fiscal consolidation continues to rely on a contraction in primary expenditures (-32.1%oya or -3.3%-pts of GDP), while primary revenues remained in the red (-4.9%oya or -1.7%-pts of GDP). As noted above, the primary spending contraction eased to 23.6%oya in April (-34.8%oya prior 3mma), while the primary revenues plunge stood at 6.4%oya (vs. -8.6%oya in March and -4.7%oya on average in the prior three months).

Breaking down by revenue category, the YTD contraction is mainly explained by lower income tax, VAT collection, and social security revenues (jointly down by 1.5%-pts of GDP). The revenue decline was partially offset by higher YTD tax collection arising from foreign trade and (temporary) “PAIS<sup>”</sup> taxes of +0.04%-pt and +0.24%-pt of GDP, respectively (see our recent note). Indeed, total <sup>‘</sup>PAIS<sup>’</sup> tax collection YTD represents more than half of the primary surplus, underlining the relevance it has gained in the overall result.

On the spending side, the oya decline continues to be widespread, with all categories showing a strong contraction YTD. The plunge in primary spending was spearheaded by social spending (-24.5%oya YTD or -1.6%-pt of GDP), followed by capital spending and consumption (down by 0.5%-pt of GDP YTD each). In all, the YTD spending adjustment was 43%, explained by the real decline in social transfers, 29% by lower capital spending and provincial transfers, another 11% by subsidies, and the remainder by consumption spending and other spending (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Factors explaining YTD expenditure decline % of total spending contraction
![](images/9f175446d306b951451b741f864b0a11d8d55bf014b9b36da99e56c2be94517a.jpg)
Source: Ministry of Treasury and J.P. Morgan

Finally, current spending was US\$1.5bn (0.22% of GDP) lower YTD on a cash vs. accrued basis, when looking from a National Administration level. This difference is mainly explained by lower subsidies spending on a cash basis due to payment delays to CAMMESA (the wholesale electricity company). Note the energy companies have accepted the government's option of paying a portion of the debt owed using Treasury bonds (which are trading below par).

We keep our forecast for a fiscal primary balance this year, while waiting for Congress to approve the Omnibus bill 2.0 and the fiscal package. As the spending contraction continues to ease, and while waiting for economic activity to recover, securing additional revenues is necessary for the fiscal consolidation program to succeed. Against this backdrop, we

continue to watch for the ongoing discussion of the Omnibus bill 2.0 and the fiscal package in the Senate. From a fiscal perspective, the most relevant changes involve a tax amnesty, as well as the re-incorporation of the income tax (both measures jointly accounting for about 0.8% of GDP in new revenues in our estimates this year). While we expect these bills to see modifications in the Senate, we still assign a high probability of approval, adding upside risks to our primary balance forecast this year. The new timeline suggests the Upper House Floor will vote on the bills in the first week of June, requiring their return to the Lower House for ratification if changes are included.

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Andeans

Chile: The CBC continued to ease monetary conditions, trimming the policy rate by 50bp to 6.0%

The national accounts confirmed the strong sequential GDP growth in 1Q24, up by 7.8%q/q, saar

We expect a substantial deceleration in 2Q24 GDP; 2024 GDP projection maintained at +2.7%y/y

## Chile: CBC cuts 50bp

In line with expectations, the CBC cut the policy rate by 50bp to 6.0%, in a unanimous decision. The Board thus decided to decelerate the easing pace again, given they cut 75bp in April, when they also changed forward guidance for a slower convergence of the policy rate to the levels deemed as neutral (note). The Council foresees that the policy rate will continue to decline, with the magnitude and timing of the reduction process dependent on the evolution of the macroeconomic scenario and its implications for the inflation trajectory.

When it comes to the statement there is not much to highlight, just the factual recognition of the CLP gains on improved terms of trade. On the external side, uncertainties around the start of the Fed easing cycle remain one of the main factors behind movements in global financial markets. Compared to the prior meeting, long-term interest rates and stock markets have moderately increased in several economies, the broad USD remains at similar levels (despite volatility), and the copper price increase stands out, driving terms of trade higher (copper up by 21% while Brent down by 6% since the April meeting). The CLP appreciated more than other comparable currencies, driven by the increase in of copper prices.

On the domestic side, it<sup>’</sup>s noted that both activity and inflation have evolved in line with the projections in the March Monetary Policy Report, with both March and April CPI aligning with the central scenario presented in April, with both headline and core standing at 3.5%.

Our base case is for a 25bp cut at the coming policy meeting, bringing the policy rate to 5.75% in June, and to 5.25% in December. The latter level would be consistent with a 50bp interest rate differential to the fed funds level, as per our US team<sup>’</sup>s base case for the Fed. Given the tight spread, any potential tweak to our colleagues<sup>’</sup> forecast would likely merit a reassessment of our own central scenario for the CBC policy rate trajectory.

## 1Q24 GDP accelerated

The national accounts report confirmed the strong sequential GDP growth in 1Q24, up by 7.8%q/q, saar, on the heels of 0.4% in 4Q23. This proved the highest expansion rate since late 2021. The combination of a stellar performance of mining (+30.6%q/q, saar ) and pretty good ex. mining momentum (+4.9% saar) explains the headline performance.

Parsing the sequential performance of the economy from the expenditure side shows a domestic demand revival, yet based on consumption amid a continued fall in gross investment. On the consumption side, household consumption gained by 4.4%ar, while public sector consumption skyrocketed by 20.4%ar. Thus, public sector consumption has increased about 31.2% from the 4Q19 level.

On the other side, investment continues to lose ground, with gross investment contracting by 0.5%ar, after the 12.3%ar plunge in 4Q. The extremely poor performance of capex continues to weigh not only on overall activity, but also on potential growth ahead. Indeed, by 1Q24, investment was basically flat when compared to 4Q19. In the same period, real GDP was 12.4% higher (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Chile domestic demand performance Index, sa, 1Q20=100
![](images/7c3151f33aab3e28af710596e20bf6c2225c5efcb988d67f7446598a128bdbce.jpg)
Finally, net exports contributed negatively to GDP in 1Q24, with both imports and exports gaining.

In all, the national accounts showed headline GDP gaining a bit less than was suggested by the monthly economic indicator (see note), though we keep our forecast mainly unchanged. Ahead, we expect a substantial deceleration in 2Q24 GDP, mainly on mining activity and compression of public sector consumption, with headline GDP logging +0.5%q/q, saar. We maintain our 2024 GDP projection at +2.7%y/y (+3.2%Q4/Q4).

## Peru

## United Kingdom

• Inflation surprises to the upside in April

Risks around August BoE cut shift from earlier to later

• Earlier than expected election called for $4 ^ { \mathrm { t h } }$ July

• Flash PMI declined in May but remains solid

Inflation delivered a huge surprise this week, driven by a spike in services pricing. The breadth of the surprise makes it hard to dismiss, although another increase on the same scale looks unlikely in subsequent months. April was probably exaggerated by annual price resets and an increase in the national living wage. Nevertheless, the CPI demonstrates that firms have a much greater ability to pass on labour cost pressures into prices than flagged in survey indicators of late - which the BoE had recently been putting quite a lot of weight on. Odds around our call for an August cut have shifted from an earlier easing to a later one. A continuation of current trends could make it hard for the BoE to cut at all this year.

The odds of June are vanishingly small. While there is more data before then, the Prime Minister this week called an early election for 4<sup>th</sup> July, just two weeks after the June MPC meeting. This is a factor that could sway a marginal vote and we note the BoE has cancelled all public speaking during the forthcoming campaign period. We continue to expect a comfortable Labour victory, and for Labour to begin the next parliament cautiously with no big market relevant policy shake ups. We expect any tax increases will initially be small. A drop in the PMI and retail sales this week reduce the upside around our 2% forecast for 2Q GDP. But with consumer confidence improving we continue to expect firmer demand this year than the BoE is expecting.

## April inflation surprises again

Headline CPI inflation for April fell from 3.2% to 2.3%oya (JPMorgan 2.0; Consensus 2.1; BoE 2.1) with core down from 4.2% to 3.9% (JPMorgan 3.4; Consensus 3.6). Services slowed from 6.0% to 5.9% (JPMorgan 5.4; Consensus 5.4; BoE 5.5). RPI was down from 4.3% to 3.3% (JPMorgan & Consensus 3.3) (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Core inflation
![](images/9078682b1a3b1f80f11cafb494cbdd400c2461d4e84f6c1e17a0b00f9b452cf0.jpg)

While we had seen upside risk around this number due to April price re-sets, we did not expect a repeat of the big surprise seen this time last year owing to the drop in energy prices and headline inflation. Strong wage growth presented the clearest case for a high CPI print, with the national living wage having increased last month. But the PMI and other pricing surveys had given no obvious indication that firms were minded to pass those pressures through so forcefully. The surprise came predominantly from services, where prices are re-set relatively infrequently and tend to be more domestic in nature. As we discuss below, the surprise was broad based across several services categories, which does seem to suggest wage costs played a factor.

Based on our seasonal adjustment, we estimate that headline prices rose 0.1%m/m in April, owing to a well flagged hefty drop in domestic energy bills, but core prices were up 0.7% over the month. There was an upside surprise to goods, where prices rose 0.4% with some help from vehicles, water and other goods, but the big beat came from services prices that shot up 0.9%m/m. Gains were very broad based, with only transport services coming in soft (education often doesn<sup>’</sup>t change). The BoE has recently started looking at measures excluding rents (rents rose 0.9% in April) but that won<sup>’</sup>t help much either due to the breadth across services. Most had expected a pop in April communication prices, where there was a very large gain of 2.7%m/m, but there was a considerable 1.9% gain in recreational and personal services (e.g. sporting, cultural) too. A category such as primary housing services (which is not rents, but plumbers, electricians, etc.) tends to get overlooked because of its small weight, but it can be symbolically important of broader pricing trends, and rose 4.4% in April.

Overall, year-ago services inflation at 5.9% is significantly higher than the BoE<sup>’</sup>s already high looking forecast of 5.5% for April. It is not quite a repeat of last year, because at least goods pricing was somewhat better behaved, but there are no real mitigating factors that we can see. Most of the underlying measures the BoE has drawn attention to look not just strong, but are strengthening (on a 3m/3m basis). The persistenceweighted supercore measure, which the BoE surprisingly stopped talking about in its May communications, rose 0.7% in April while the 3m/3m annualised pace shot up from 5.4% to 6.0% based on our seasonal adjustment (the 3m, saar rate is 6.6%) (Figure 2).

Figure 2: BoE persistence weighted "supercore" inflation %, excludes accommodation, airfares, education, non-private rents
![](images/346e04dba8a9d57c4aa0d421c6d675fb31f22a3bf2c815f3c5bd796ee663db3a.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan calculations

Our new forecast anticipates no real payback in May, but also doesn<sup>’</sup>t assume any kind of repeat of what happened in the April data. This is consistent with what happened last year. We have year-ago headline inflation falling to 2% (instead of below) in May. Core remains well above 3% and this eventually drags headline back above the target from 4Q. There are risks in both directions, but the experience from last year suggests that when firms are feeling confident enough to pass costs on they can continue to do so in subsequent months. The biggest risk at present comes from stronger growth, which is running well above BoE expectations. That could increase business pricing power and tilt inflation risks to the upside.

This is only one month<sup>’</sup>s data, but it is enough of a surprise to suggest that the inflation process is not tracking as the BoE had expected. It had recently appeared to gain confidence about the outlook despite no obvious underlying improvement in the wage and price data. There is still another labour market and CPI report to come before the June meeting, but it is difficult for us to see what that could realistically do to leave most members feeling confident about cutting in June specifically, especially when key members such as Broadbent have spoken in more general terms about a summer easing. We stick with our August call but believe the risks have clearly tilted back towards a later cut, and the talking point now will be whether the BoE can ease at all this year.

## Sunak calls for election for 4<sup>th</sup> July

The Prime Minister this week announced the imminent dissolution of Parliament and called a general election for Thursday 4<sup>th</sup> July. This is earlier than the September and November candidate dates that had looked more likely, although a summer poll was an outside chance.

The motivation for the somewhat earlier election isn<sup>’</sup>t entirely clear, as it removes the opportunity to hold another fiscal event later this year. But there are significant fiscal constraints that bring two-sided risks with that option. There may be some advantage to holding the vote when headline inflation is temporarily hovering around 2% and growth is improving.

Nevertheless, it<sup>’</sup>s unusual for a government to call an election earlier than necessary when trailing so heavily in the polls, some 20 points behind Labour. But polling momentum is weak for the Conservatives, and the Reform party is gaining ground to further split its core voter base. Holding a vote any later could result in a larger electoral defeat for the government.

A Labour victory looks very likely, with leading psephologists arguing that the government<sup>’</sup>s most realistic hope is for a hung Parliament. But even in that unlikely event, Labour would find it a lot easier to form a coalition or rule by minority than the Conservatives. It would still likely be the largestsingle party, and has more Parliamentary allies.

We recently published a note on the election and the implications of a likely Labour government (see “An uphill struggle for Labour in government<sup>”</sup>). Labour will be heavily fiscally constrained, and will seek to support growth with supply side reforms rather than risky deficit financed spending. The planned path for next year is already fiscal tightening, and an incoming government is unlikely to deviate from that straight after an election victory.

Labour is to some extent likely to raise revenue in order to deliver on the tightening currently planned, and at the same time to ease the burden on spending restraint. But we expect any compositional adjustment will be moderate initially, without increases to the main tax rates. The UK tax take is historically elevated, higher taxes jar with a stated strategy to lift longer-term growth, and Labour is seeking to retain its current high level of support from the public while reassuring businesses.

## Flash PMI steps back in May

The flash PMI declined from 54.1 to 52.8 in May. We<sup>’</sup>d expected a drop to 53.7, and so the survey tempers some upside risk around the growth forecast. But even as it stands, the survey is still pointing towards growth in the region of 1.5-2.0% compared to our 2.0% forecast for 2Q. The PMI can be volatile, and momentum in the monthly GDP data into 2Q is strong with real income growth robust. The details of the PMI hint at a strengthening in employment, which suggests the growth upswing may be generating fresh labour demand. This could be a challenge to the MPC<sup>’</sup>s optimism over productivity, although the jobs pickup does look very modest. Future output expectations also rose, and manufacturing stepped up to partially offset a services driven decline. Input prices fell back, following the April boost from the national living wage increase. Output prices moved down, which is an observation that has given some comfort to the MPC regarding inflation persistence. But the CPI data on the week show there is more pricing power than the surveys currently suggest (Figure 3).

## Emerging Europe

• Türkiye: CBRT keeps rates on hold, tightens via macroprudential measures

## Türkiye: CBRT keeps rates on hold, tightens via macroprudential measures

The CBRT kept the policy rate on hold at 50%, in line with our expectation and market consensus. The CBRT kept its hawkish bias by reiterating it is ready to tighten in case it foresees significant deterioration in inflation. The CBRT noted that the underlying trend of monthly inflation registered a limited decline in April. It also emphasized that recent indicators point to a slowdown in domestic demand compared to the first quarter by noting increased consumption goods imports in April. Headline inflation likely peaked at 74.8% oya (3.0% m/m) this month due to unfavorable base effects driven by natural gas prices. We expect a sharp fall towards 51% oya in August on the back of favorable base effects and a slowdown in the monthly inflation momentum. We maintain our end 2024 inflation forecast at 43.5% as the fiscal policy is likely to be more supportive of disinflation in 2H24.

The CBRT hiked the RRR for TRY deposits and the FX-protected lira deposit accounts (KKM) to mop up excess lira liquidity, as expected. Excess lira liquidity emerged in May on the back of surging demand for Turkish lira assets from Turkish residents and international investors. Excess lira liquidity pushed the overnight interbank lira rate down to 47% as of 22 May, from 53% on 10 May. To sterilize excess lira liquidity, the CBRT hiked lira RRR from 8% to 12% for lira deposits up to 3 months, from 0% to 8% with maturity longer than 3 months. The CBRT also increased RRR from 25% to 33% for KKM up to 6 months, and from 10% to 22% for KKM longer than 6 months. We calculate the lira liquidity withdrawal of more than TRY 550bn which would be enough to mop up excess lira liquidity as CBRT absorbed TRY 516bn on 22 May. Removal of excess lira liquidity should push the overnight interbank lira rate to increase towards 50% or more, from 47% before the meeting. As of 17 May, the average deposit rate up to 3 months fell to 63.7% (Figure 1) on excess liquidity. Now we believe that lira deposit rates are likely to rise again following these macroprudential measures. A renewed rise in lira deposit rates might accelerate KKM unwinding, and accelerate de-dollarization among Turkish residents.

The CBRT introduced RRR to curb FX loan growth in line with our expectations. FX loan growth accelerated as corporates want to substitute their working capital needs through FX credits. The CBRT imposed a monthly 2% loan growth cap for FX credits and introduced an RRR for the loan amount exceeding this limit. On the back of this measure, we expect FX loan growth to slow from 55% towards 27% or less in annualized growth momentum.

Figure 1: Policy rate and market rates
![](images/a3c92c984b344ea4973fd41ceb217fcb22b0f3dbdea29d24a217992d8b1a63b1.jpg)

The CBRT\`s FX reserves sharply improved in April and May. The CBRT concentrated on improving the composition of FX reserves by unwinding FX swaps with local banks in April and May. The CBRT reduced its FX swap stock by \$35.6bn in April and May to \$22.3bn as of 22 May. The CBRT\`s net FX position (i.e. net foreign assets excluding FX swaps) improved by \$58bn in April and May to its highest level in the last 4 years at -\$16bn as of 22 May (Figure 2). This was mainly driven by the de-dollarization among Turkish residents (\$14.6bn) and foreign inflows (\$18.2bn inflows into offshore FX swaps, \$5.8bn into local government bonds, \$0.4bn into equities) between 29 March and 17 May following March municipal elections. We expect the CBRT to continue to rebuild its FX reserves thanks to de-dollarization among Turkish residents, foreign portfolio inflows, and increased seasonal tourism revenues in the coming months.

## South Africa

Polls ahead of National Elections on 29 May show the ANC in the driving seat, though likely below 50%

MPC to keep policy rate steady at 8.25% with little forward guidance

• Inflation surprised marginally lower at 5.2%oya

Election polls continue to point to the ANC losing its outright majority, with which it governed for the last three decades, when national and provincial elections are held on 29 May. Yet, the likely coalition structure now seems considerably more feasible as fewer minor (and very likely no major) coalition partners are necessary. After the emergence of the MK party, polls initially captured a reduction in ANC support with surveys dropping to 37%-39%, but since then have broadly stabilized at 43.7% since mid-May (Figure 1).

Consumer prices
Figure 1: ANC support in surveys (no raw polls)
![](images/02a9f78a1ca7e0f7a9ba7baf31ea1e774464d2c7cc03dfe63000b8b30495bf40.jpg)
Source: J.P.Morgan calculations, SRF, IPSOS, Brenthurst, eNCA

Our point-estimate (based on polls and by-elections) would currently place ANC support at 45.2%. If correct, this could significantly simplify the coalition structure. An arrangement with just the IFP (4.8%) alone could suffice, or at most one or two very closely aligned parties where a working relationship is already established (e.g. GOOD). Previously, our base case was similarly that the ANC most likely would form a coalition with minor parties, and potentially a handful of these small parties would be required to obtain 6%-7% of votes to bring the coalition to above 50%. While the point-estimate of ANC support has improved somewhat, the margin of error of surveys likely is considerable, as we illustrated in a prior note. We think this places ANC support in the range of 42.7% to 47.7%.

## SARB likely firmly on hold at 8.25%

We expect the MPC to retain a cautious tone with a hawkish bias when it announces its policy rate decision one day after National Elections. The SARB very likely will keep the policy rate unchanged at 8.25%. Upside inflation risks have tempered somewhat over the last two months as food price pressures from El Niño have remained contained, despite earlier fears. In addition, the currency has strengthened somewhat to USD/ZAR 18.44 and the recent inflation outcome for April was a touch below expectations with core inflation contained.

However, the SARB likely will be hesitant to reduce the policy rate until risks around global inflation dynamics have improved further and the FOMC is closer to implementing a rate cut. Moreover, the SARB retains a lingering concern around local services inflation and would like to see inflation more securely on the path to 4.5%, not least as a lowering of the inflation target likely will take place over the medium term. We do not expect significant changes to the macroeconomic projections of the SARB.

## Australia and New Zealand

The minutes to the RBA’s May meeting confirmed the Board considered a hike

We think Australia’s monthly CPI indicator rose 3.4%oya in April

The RBNZ remained on hold at the May meeting, but flagged the Board discussed hiking

• We expect NZ Treasury will forecast a FY25 deficit of NZ\$12bn in next week’s budget

The minutes from the RBA<sup>’</sup>s May policy decision confirmed the Board discussed the merits of hiking rates versus remaining on hold, with members opting for stability. The guidance was similar to previous iterations with the minutes noting, “it was difficult either to rule in or rule out future changes in the cash rate target.<sup>”</sup>

The bulk of the commentary was even-handed, though the tone surrounding the inflation discussion skewed hawkish, an expected development given the CPI upgrades marked through in May<sup>’</sup>s Statement on Monetary Policy (SoMP). Although the RBA raised its sights on near-term inflation, the return to the target band was left unchanged (2H25), an outcome the minutes note was, “predicated on a technical assumption for the cash rate that was noticeably higher than previously assumed<sup>”</sup>. This is a subtle acknowledgement that inflation pressures are more acute than previously assumed and will require monetary conditions to stay tighter for longer, a dynamic consistent with our view the RBA is likely to remain on hold until early 2025.

While current guidance (“not ruling anything in or out") gives the Bank optionality and the ability to respond to the incoming data, the comment highlighting the Board has “limited tolerance for inflation returning to target later than 2026<sup>”</sup> puts an official clock (admittedly long-dated) on the inflation trajectory. In our view, it would take a very large shock to see the 2026 inflation forecast push above 3%, though the decision to use calendar guidance is interesting and highlights the Bank<sup>’</sup>s resolve in returning inflation to the target band.

## Monthly CPI moderating slowly

We expect Australian monthly CPI increased 3.4%oya in April, implying a 0.5%m/m rise. The March CPI print was stronger than expected (+3.5%oya), reflecting broad strength in the basket, with the core measures all rising compared to February. Tracking data for April suggest clothing and footwear, health, and recreation/culture prices should be strong. Offsetting this should be some weakness in furniture prices. The food and housing subgroups comprise a large weight in the basket and our tracking estimates suggest some weakness in both components. But these subgroups are harder to forecast than others month to month, so we attach some upside risk to our headline forecast.

Figure 1: Monthly headline and ex. volatile items inflation %oya
![](images/69125a1b1a26e17ecb76ac6e76b8d9f856f7adc090c7e85f14067235a9cf169f.jpg)

## RBNZ treading water

The RBNZ was on hold this week at 5.50% as we and consensus expected. The forecast set and statement maintained the high for long message (Figure 2) which, while an expected one, is looking fatigued in the face of ongoing weak data and a likely return to the inflation target band in 2H. The staff's OCR forecast is still showing no easing until 2Q25, and has been nudged up 10-30bp. The minutes also said a hike was discussed, though this prospect has been argued for a year without delivery and hasn<sup>’</sup>t looked plausible in the face of the data.

Figure 2: RBNZ OCR forecasts (Feb vs May MPS)
![](images/bb89b69e0897d1e1c548fdc12401d8a3f5f8a8bbb1c65d29d1d818bae91de204.jpg)

In our preview, we had flagged that the committee would not pre-emptively endorse the case for easing, as it seems to want the return to the inflation target to be all but locked in before pivoting. In supporting this message, concern about non-tradables/services inflation, alongside downward revisions to potential output, have been a way to downplay the significance of weaker growth forecasts in the past, and this was pretty much the mix of the forecast set. Given the committee<sup>’</sup>s prerogative, the caution about sticky services is not overly surprising, and potential growth tweaks have been a go-to communications device in the past, when the RBNZ wants to prevent the market from getting ahead of itself in pricing easing.

Like many central banks, the leadership's desire to convey vigilance stems from fears of above-normal domestic/services inflation becoming entrenched. Though the underlying concern is a genuine one, the evidence has been moving in the other direction. GDP, measures of capacity use, and inflation expectations are all either weak or back in normal ranges, and only base effects are keeping annual inflation up until 3Q.

Governor Orr seemed to downplay the OCR forecast path to an extent in the press conference, stating the committee “does not think in that space<sup>”</sup> regarding a very precise expected or committed future path, with each meeting providing opportunity to start afresh. He also mentioned that the committee did not want to declare victory on inflation “just before the finish line.<sup>”</sup> Despite all the worry about persistence, we think this gives a clear sense that the pivot is close, but there won<sup>’</sup>t be many clues from the committee along the way (our forecast is 3Q).

## NZ tracking a bigger budget deficit

Next week<sup>’</sup>s 2024 NZ Budget carries perceived implications for both the bond market and monetary policy, particularly since the recent BPS provided less detail than usual. The last official update in HYEFU projected a deficit of NZ\$6.1bn in FY25. We expect this will be increased to NZ\$12bn given downgrades to the medium-term revenue outlook and recent growth performance. The economy has been weak enough for long enough that structural budget parameters like productivity and potential output could be shifted lower, and this implies a weaker fiscal position even with an unchanged policy set.

The mix of endogenous weakness from revenues/potential growth vs. spending cuts will dictate the implications for monetary policy, but the picture is unlikely to be clear until the RBNZ has made its own assessment. We don<sup>’</sup>t expect new fiscal measures and spending cuts to fully offset revenue downgrades in FY25 alone. So, while the DMO<sup>’</sup>s bond supply projections have been relatively elevated compared to the running deficit and maturity profile for some years, we still look for issuance numbers to be biased a little higher from the current baseline of NZ\$32bn gross for FY25 (NZ\$17bn net).

## Greater China

• Hong Kong: unemployment rate unchanged at 3.0%

• CPI inflation softened significantly in April

• Taiwan: Significant upside surprise in export orders, reinforcing constructive external demand conditions

• IP beat expectations with a modest gain

• Next week: China PMIs, Hong Kong trade and retail sales, Taiwan GDP final

Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate stayed at 3.0% sa for the three months ending April (Figure 1). Total employment rose 5.7k, to 3,694.1k, while the labor force rose 7.7k, to 3,807.8k. Seasonally adjusted, total employment rose 0.5%m/m sa, while the labor force rose 0.4%m/m sa. The underemployment rate stayed at 1.1%. Our seasonal adjustment analysis suggests that both the labor force and employment have been on the rise for three months in a row.

Figure 1: Hong Kong unemployment rate
![](images/a667119e41d881b11bbe25f34a554f18b2d28fb6c33b653791c8b973fc4d48d6.jpg)

According to the HK C&SD, the unemployment rates of various sectors remained low in general in February-April. Meanwhile, we estimate that employment in finance & insurance, real estate and professional services sectors fell 0.6k (or +0.5%m/m sa), reversing the 0.3%m/m sa loss in January-March. Employment in retail, accommodation and food services sectors fell 1.3k (or flat in m/m sa terms) after the 0.1%m/m sa loss in January-March. Note that the aforementioned sectors accounted for 22.8% and 14.1% of HK total employment in February-April, respectively. Overall, the city<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate has returned to the lowest level since the onset of the pandemic, and we expect it to remain around 3% in the near term.

## CPI inflation softened

Hong Kong SAR<sup>’</sup>s headline CPI inflation softened to +1.1%oya in April, compared to +2.0%oya in March (Figure 2). Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that headline CPI fell 0.4%m/m sa in April (vs. 0.0%m/m sa in March). In the breakdown, we estimate that food prices rose 0.1%m/m sa in

April, with meals away from home rising 0.1%m/m sa. Another major component of CPI, housing-related prices, fell 1.2%m/m sa in April, likely due to the substitution effect from the housing market, as the removal of additional stamp duties has stimulated housing transactions lately. Miscellaneous service prices fell 0.4%m/m sa in April (vs. 0.0%m/m sa in March).

Figure 2: Hong Kong headline CPI
![](images/859f38825977c057bc13005e8e59cc5449cd4409522113badbf78ce8a1951f2f.jpg)

Prices of electricity, gas and water fell 0.6%m/m sa in April, adding to the 1.6%m/m sa decline in March. Note that CLP and HK Electric have reduced tariffs since January. Excluding food and energy, core CPI grew 1.3%oya (or -0.7%m/m sa) in April. Netting out the effects of all one-off government relief measures, the composite CPI rose 0.9%oya in April (vs. 1.0%oya in March). Looking ahead, we maintain our view that overall inflation in HK should stay moderate in the near term.

## Taiwan export orders beat expectations

Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s export orders peaked in 1Q22 and trended down steadily through 1H23, reflecting the fading of the Covid-related WFH tech demand boom, slowing in the global manufacturing sector, and de-stocking pressure in the global tech sector. From mid-2023 onwards, the level of export orders (in sa terms) seemed to have stabilized. Since early this year, Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s export orders appear to show signs of a steady upturn. In April, export orders came in significantly above expectations, rising 10.8%oya. We estimate that, seasonally adjusted, export orders rose 4.9%m/m, sa, with strong sequential trend growth at 31.7%3m/3m saar.

Tech orders rose 4.5%m/m sa in April, with strong sequential trend growth at 50.1%3m/3m saar (Figure 3), reflecting a broad-based upturn in orders for electronics products as well as information and communication products. Non-tech orders grew a modest 4.2% 3m/3m saar in April, led by machinery orders (3.9%). By source of demand, orders from the US (+41.6%3m/3m saar in April) and Europe (+105.5%) have led overall orders growth in recent months, while orders from

China/ HKSAR showed a moderate gain (+11.4%). Orders from the ASEAN region came down (-45.4%) from a high base.

Figure 3: Taiwan export orders sequential growth %3m/3m saar
![](images/ea73ea42274a412a27734204e69aa10fbf1dd0843f3b2aa691842d24e50ac642.jpg)
Source: MOEA, J.P. Morgan

We look for a further steady upward trend in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s tech exports in the coming quarters. A constructive outlook on global goods demand, supported by resilient corporate sector fundamentals and a solid labor income trend, bodes well for Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s export sector activity. Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s tech export orders have underperformed actual tech exports since early 2023. This likely reflects the boost of AI-related tech demand (largely produced in Taiwan manufacturing plants), while a significant share of export orders involves other traditional tech products manufactured offshore. As such, the latest solid upturn in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s tech export orders likely suggests a nearterm solid growth outlook not only for Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s tech exports (led by AI-related products), but also for a broader demand increase for other tech products, including those manufactured from mainland China.

## IP grew a modest 1.5%m/m sa

Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s industrial production came in stronger than expected in April, growing 14.6%oya or 1.5%m/m sa. It compares to our forecast of 13.5%oya. The underlying trend remained solid at 6.9%3m/3m saar. In the breakdown, tech-related manufacturing IP grew a modest 0.6%m/m sa in April, following the 0.5%m/m sa fall in March. Smoothing out monthly volatility, tech-related IP showed solid sequential growth at 12.8%3m/3m saar in April (Figure 4), following the strong 4Q23 growth (38.6%q/q saar). Non-tech industrial activity rose 4.5%m/m sa in April, more than offsetting the 2.8% m/m sa decline in March, including a rise in production of base metals (+3.4%m/m sa), as well as machinery and equipment manufacturing (+9.4%m/m sa).

Figure 4: Taiwan tech and nontech manufacturing IP (level) Index, 2011=100, sa, 3mma
![](images/23d39a7d7179b507be7b813c659a2c20919ce00542c0751605b8940f4621fb28.jpg)

Regarding Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s inventory conditions, recall that manufacturing inventory had trended down notably since early 2023 as domestic manufacturers made significant efforts to cut inventory (especially for tech). The significant inventory correction was followed by a moderate upturn in 4Q23 (when manufacturing inventory rose 11.6%3m/3m saar). Going into early this year, overall manufacturing inventory appeared to be stabilizing, with a modest gain of 1.8%m/m sa in March, adding to the 0.9% uptick in February, with the underlying sequential trend rising a marginal 0.4%3m/3m saar in March.

## Korea

• May MPC confirmed our view from April meeting

• Consumer sentiment and household credit growth moderated

• Exports turned up in mid-May to suggest 2Q nominal exports growth recovery

• PPI continued to moderate in April

The Bank of Korea maintained its base policy rate at 3.5% as expected. The BoK<sup>’</sup>s revised its macroeconomic forecast in a hawkish direction, raising its annual GDP growth forecast and noting upside risk to the inflation outlook. At the same time, the post-MPC communication suggested that the BoK may ease the policy rate within the year if inflation stability is confirmed. Such May meeting details are in line with our interpretation and view from the April meeting, and we continue to expect the first rate cut to be in 4Q24. Economic data this week also support a soft landing of macro conditions, with a moderation in the consumer sentiment index, household credit growth, and producer prices. At the same time, high-frequency data suggest stable nominal exports growth in 2Q after a brief correction in 1Q.

## Expecting a 25bp cut in 4Q24

After the April meeting we changed our BoK policy forecast to a later and shallower rate cut cycle, anticipating the BoK<sup>’</sup>s reluctance to move midyear and upside bias on the GDP growth and inflation forecasts. The data after the April meeting confirmed the upside bias in macro data including the surprisingly strong 1Q24 growth, and the BoK has not yet clearly signaled a near-term pivot. To be sure, the official MPC statement in May added clauses on enhancing the growth impulse and higher upside risk for inflation, and the governor acknowledged that uncertainty on the timing of the first rate cut has increased.

That said, the hawkish forecast revision was not strong enough to delay the first rate cut expectation to 2025. While the BoK revised up 2024<sup>’</sup>s annual GDP growth forecast to 2.5%y/y from 2.1%, the annual inflation forecast for 2024 was unchanged at 2.6%y/y. The governor commented that the strong 2024 annual GDP growth may have limited spillover to inflation, as the growth would be led by net exports, not mainly by domestic demand. The MPC members<sup>’</sup> dot chart remains unchanged as well, implying a gradually normalizing policy stance, as one of six members is open to the possibility of a rate cut in the near term.

Looking forward, we still see upside risk to the BoK<sup>’</sup>s revised GDP growth forecast for this year (JPMe 2.8% vs BoK 2.5%), but we expect lower core (JPMe 1.7% vs BoK 2.0%)

and headline inflation (JPMe 1.7% vs. BoK 2.1%) for 2025. In our view, while the growth outlook may need to be upgraded further, the policy message from the stronger GDP growth may soften if trend stabilization of inflation in 4Q24 provides comfort. Therefore, we continue to expect the first rate cut action in 4Q24, to be followed by 25bp cuts in 2Q25 and 4Q25 to reach 2.75% by end-2025.

## Moderation of CSI and HH credit growth

Korea<sup>’</sup>s consumer sentiment fell 2.3pts to 98.4 in May, after staying flat for a month in April; it has declined from 101.6 in January and 101.9 in February, returning to the 98.4 average seen in 4Q23. That said, the sentiment index is still much higher than its lows during the 2022-23 period of peak inflation and loan rates (Figure 1). This conforms with our view that domestic demand<sup>’</sup>s growth contribution will moderate after 1Q24<sup>’</sup>s strength but is unlikely to slip into recession this year.

Figure 1: Consumer sentiment and household credit growth %q/q, saar Index, 100=long-term avg.
![](images/66b28bae3dc30159a3de060d4efab8a27fdd3e7c1cb8d30abeae372a0bc3f5da.jpg)

Meanwhile, household (HH) credit increased 1.6%oya in 1Q24, slightly faster than 1.0% in the previous quarter. Sequentially, HH credit rose 1.8%q/q, saar (sa by JPM) keeping its moderate pace following growth of 2.1% in 3Q23 and 1.5% in 4Q23, after contracting by 0.9% per quarter in 3Q22- 1Q23 (Figure 1). Recent stabilization of HH credit growth at a pace (1.6%oya in 1Q24) slower than nominal GDP growth (5.8%oya in 4Q23, the latest available data) may imply a continued de-leveraging of HH credit as a share of GDP.

## Exports turned up in mid-May

Customs exports flow turned up in mid-May after the earlier 10-day flow<sup>’</sup>s decline. If seasonally adjusted, the first 20 days<sup>’</sup> exports rose 1.9%m/m, sa, in the 20-day flow vs. falling 2.7% in the 10-day flow, suggesting that the three-month trend growth may turn up to 7.0%3m/3m, saar (Figure 2). Customs exports growth should be less than the strong 24.1%3m/3m, saar, growth in December 2023, yet should continue to expand, buoyed by the tech sector<sup>’</sup>s exports growth.

Figure 2: Korea customs exports
![](images/c531968191427b82a439dad449797b2fd0fa79731d6596334c4516ab27838384.jpg)

## PPI continued to moderate in April

The producer price index edged down 0.1%m/m, sa in April. Sequentially, headline PPI inflation declined to 1.5%3m/3m, saar, in the month, after hovering around a 2%-3% pace in the previous four months, continuing to slow from a 4.8% peak in October. The core PPI led the moderation of trend, yet going forward the energy PPI should also reflect the recent decline in global oil prices.

## ASEAN

● ASEAN continues to benefit from trade shifts between China and US

Malaysia and Vietnam primary beneficiaries of trade shift

• Indonesia benefits from metals trade with China

Trade patterns with Thailand and Philippines remain unclear

As regional supply chains evolve accelerated by US diversification from China, ASEAN sits as a beneficiary of this trade re-orientation. There are two elements of this diversification, via a deepening of supply chains in ASEAN, which benefits mainly Vietnam and Malaysia and a re-orientation of trade through transshipment, with Hong Kong being the most affected. However, limited data on re-exports preclude a more comprehensive analysis of the beneficiaries of these transshipment flows.

It appears that industrial deepening is progressing in Vietnam and Malaysia and this should also benefit the ancillary traderelated services. In higher-end manufacturing, this is focused mainly in tech-related goods. For Malaysia, although there has been a positive shift in the customs trade balance, this has not accrued in the trade balance as reflected in the balance of payments due to the large share of transshipment-related trade. In the case of Thailand, its production focus has reduced the benefit from the tech-related shift. By contrast, Indonesia has seen a rise in its net exports position due to idiosyncratic factors and not so much a result of the trade reorientation. For the Philippines, its manufacturing share has been declining and suggests that its imports are more destined for domestic demand than for exports to the rest of the world. This trend would imply an ongoing deterioration of its external balances as the industrial sector hollows out.

## US-China trade spillovers

Over the past six years, there has been a notable shift in China<sup>’</sup>s export share, with export shares to the US and Hong Kong declining but expanding to ASEAN, the rest of the world and LATAM. Part of the rise in export shares in our view likely reflects re-orientation of final demand from the US while in ASEAN, it more likely reflects the supply chain response to US-China trade uncertainty (Figure 1). This is evident in the sharp rise in China<sup>’</sup>s exports shares to ASEAN between 2018 and 2022 and from ASEAN to the US which offset the drop in China<sup>’</sup>s exports to the US, suggesting some reconfiguring of trade flows across the region (Figure 2). This recent reconfiguration adds to the longer-term trend of diversification away from China to ASEAN.

Figure 1: Mainland China shift in export share % pt. shift in export share, 1Q2024 less 2018
![](images/5d577aac0932a23afa008c5764b2657dbb8479067cb3b95ea5a3a96e93da873c.jpg)

Figure 2: Shift in US import share % shift in import share, 1Q2024 less 2018
![](images/664e4441f652801d721a5add329ff0e34a8712bb162a1db0e807e02d9a23758c.jpg)

Figure 3: China shift in export shares
![](images/a0336ea9b1261aad1e85d26ec175dcc7a04fbf94af42c03df16ec38a8b13e392.jpg)

To some extent, the experience here resonated with the response to the Multi Fiber Agreement, with transshipment via unconstrained third countries mitigating the impact of trade restrictions. The inference here is that transshipment flows have likely also moved away from Greater China to ASEAN/LATAM (Figure 3).

## Figuring the ASEAN beneficiaries

Nonetheless, we can proximately gauge the degree of production shift through changes in manufacturing shares. Malaysia and Vietnam have seen a steady rise in manufacturing shares in contrast with Indonesia, the Philippines, and Thailand, where they have been declining steadily since 2010 (Figure 4). Surprisingly, Vietnam<sup>’</sup>s manufacturing share remains comparable to Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s and Thailand<sup>’</sup>s and suggests that there could be room for further capacity expansion.

Figure 4: ASEAN manufacturing
![](images/f90b660439d1dd247b99ad1f40f8b94529b841d9a97f1a956ce26700cf519e79.jpg)

However, given the co-mingling of production and transshipment flows in the trade data, disentangling the two is not straightforward without more granular re-export data.

## FDI flows up selectively in ASEAN

That being said, FDI flows to ASEAN have surged and this has been due in large part to Singapore (Figure 5). For the rest of the ASEANs, FDI flows have not exhibited unusual gains.

Figure 5: Gross FDI inflows

![](images/c0d31224e7cd64bee312aa8a59e31772043646b0acd0d5621f37eec4477238a2.jpg)

Figure 6: ASEAN gross FDI
![](images/3b70eb4b449ff3a66172445071b3f920e7376097b466ecd1a73e4c29271f3630.jpg)
Source: National sources, details on request

Gross direct investment inflows reveal a similar dynamic, with FDI in Malaysia surging in 2021/22 but down last year even as FDI in Vietnam slowed though holding at a still high level (Figure 6). Thus, it seems from the manufacturing and FDI data that Vietnam and Malaysia have been the key beneficiaries of production relocation while the evidence for production deepening in the other ASEAN economies remains tentative.

## Triangular trade with China

The rise in production capacity should also be evident in the rise in trade surpluses as the net value-added from manufacturing rises. Moreover, as has been evident in China and techrelated production, supply-chain-related trade tends to be triangular, with a rise net imports from China—which consist largely of intermediates—offset by rising net exports of finished goods excluding energy (Figure 7).

Figure 7: ASEAN trade balance
![](images/cb95264d1a4a06626ae609e2a5c7e157319bff3cdd03ac6af97a5b4eb5f339da.jpg)

The nuances in trade flows thus convey idiosyncratic dynamics and provide some insights of the economic trends. As noted, the two key beneficiaries of the production relocation are Malaysia and Vietnam and the evolution of their respective trade balances convey similar economic narratives. In the case of Malaysia, the trade surplus has surged due to rising value added and also from transshipment flows. Beyond a beneficiary of the US-China trade war in 2018, Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s manufacturing sector has experienced a boost in demand stemming from the upturn in the global tech cycle.

Figure 8: Malaysia trade balance
![](images/982c48cd6907ac6b170889c7762971e1941916c48f313c705d9df4985a2496a5.jpg)

The country has cemented its position in the middle of the semiconductor value chain which has in turn propelled the trade balance to record highs more recently (Figure 8). The bulk of the expansion in exports more recently stems from Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s increasingly strong position as both a global distribution and processing hub due to its geographical advantage. Interestingly, as there is no change in ownership of these exports, the goods surplus on a BOP basis remains trend-like despite the stellar performance of the trade balance on a customs basis. Thus, even though approved foreign capital investment surged last year, it is difficult to discern if the bulk of the investment is destined for the expansion in distribution facilities or for deepening manufacturing. In any case, manufacturing GDP is likely to benefit in the medium term.

Similarly, Vietnam has seen a surge in its trade surplus ex China and energy, though this has been offset by an increase in net imports from China (Figure 9), which have risen mainly in tech parts and industrial materials to be processed for final production (Figure 10). Imports also increased in machinery and equipment, likely reflecting a move of production capacity from China to Vietnam as a part of supply-chain shifts. The rise in net imports from China has accelerated since 2019, possibly stimulated by escalated US-China trade tensions and stringent quarantine requirements in China since the onset of the pandemic. That said, the narrow margin of net exports suggests that supply-chain relocations from China to Vietnam are yet concentrated in low-value added production such as final assembly, where Vietnam has a comparative advantage with its lower labor cost.

Figure 9: Vietnam trade balance
![](images/14f02573fb998a8c3546bbdb4229118782408b098419d7a93b14c2d358a32928.jpg)

Figure 10: Indonesia net exports of manufactured goods US\$bn, 3mma, ar
![](images/e34413f089cf46720a4023e9dbf93fc0b7c3e5227d4ae3eb374f83727876a55b.jpg)
In Indonesia, there has been a shift towards downstreaming of metals production and could reflect the offshoring of production of basic manufactures as China seeks to achieve carbon

neutrality by 2060 (Figure 10). If so, this trend should continue, providing increasing support for Indonesia<sup>’</sup>s trade surplus.

## India

RBI transfers much higher than expected dividends to the centre

• This windfall increases fiscal degrees of freedom for the government

• Next week: 1Q24 GDP at 7% oya

General elections to conclude on 1<sup>st</sup> June; results on 4<sup>th</sup> June

RBI approved a transfer of funds to the Central government significantly exceeding both the budgeted and last year<sup>’</sup>s dividend. This increase likely stems from higher interest on domestic and foreign holdings in addition to high gross forex sales with specific details available in June. The unexpected windfall enhances the new government<sup>’</sup>s fiscal flexibility.

Separately, India<sup>’</sup>s 1Q24 GDP print to be released next week is expected to be at 7% oya driven by stronger than expected manufacturing as well as public investment even as agricultural growth is likely to remain subdued.

## Centre gets large windfall from RBI

The RBI board approved a transfer of Rs.2.1 trn (0.6% of GDP) as the annual dividend to the Central Government which was significantly higher than the budgeted Rs. 1.0 trn (0.3% of GDP) and last year<sup>’</sup>s dividend of 0.3% of GDP (Figure 1). The increase is likely to be on account of some combination of higher interest from domestic and foreign sources, and from exchange gains from foreign currency transactions. The breakdown of which component accounted for how much would only be available in June. As we discuss below, this larger-than-expected windfall significantly improves the new government<sup>’</sup>s fiscal degrees of freedom this year, and will likely have on imprint in the budget math when the next administration presents its first budget in July.

Figure 1: RBI Dividend to Government
![](images/64cc5ff4f6b5bedf1a32921151f36ea8e0e06816ff68409774de9cb13c6947ac.jpg)
Source: RBI, J.P. Morgan

## Risks of a lower-than-expected fiscal deficit in 2023-24

Before we delve deeper into the current year<sup>’</sup>s fiscal, we need to step back and get a sense of fiscal dynamics for the year that just ended, 2023-24.

Recall, the Interim Budget in February had pegged the FY24 deficit at 5.8% of GDP. However, three months of fiscal accounting were still left in the fiscal year when the budget was presented.

Since then, total expenditures—led by revenue expenditure— are lower than what the interim Budget had implied even as gross tax collections have kept pace. In particular, total expenditures grew at 2.6% oya in January and February, lower than the 4.8% implied for the January-March quarter. Meanwhile, gross tax revenues in January and February grew at 8.2% oya, very close to the estimate of 8.1% for January to March (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Tax and expenditure growth
![](images/e9fdcb825222a8a5b2d08688149859333cf5f52785c0f78d8e15117676b7a601.jpg)

This undershoot on expenditure creates risk that fiscal deficit in 2023-24 could be lower than the revised estimate of 5.8% of GDP, though data for March is still awaited.

Indeed, the government was holding cash balances of \~Rs. 1.7-2 trn (\~0.6% of GDP) at the fiscal year-end (March 31) vis-à-vis a budgeted estimate of 0-0.1% of GDP balance. While this suggests that the government could have accrued some savings and the FY24 fiscal deficit could come in lower, there are many moving parts (like larger cash balances could reflect improved state fiscal, or centre attracting larger funds from other budget financing sources like small savings and state provident fund) and hence the actual fiscal deficit would only be known in next week<sup>’</sup>s data release.

## Increased fiscal degrees of freedom this year

For the current year, a positive dividend surprise of 0.3% of GDP from the RBI increases fiscal degrees of freedom. Recall, the interim budget targets a large consolidation this year, from 5.8\$ of GDP to 5.1% of GDP. Furthermore, all of this is achieved by reducing revenue expenditures by 0.7% of GDP. In contrast, capex is budgeted healthily.

Therefore, the 0.3% of GDP windfall could be used to (a) buffer the cut in revenue expenditures, (b) offset any revenue shortfalls, especially on asset sales that are budgeted at 0.3% of GDP, but whose achievement has been softer in recent years, or (c) to consolidate further and thereby reduce market borrowings.

## A less contractionary consolidation?

One concerns for 2024-25 was that the fiscal consolidation would be achieved largely by cutting revenue expenditures. However, to the extent that the consolidation is achieved by an increase in the RBI dividend (from 0.3% of GDP in FY24 to 0.6% of FDP in FY25) the contractionary impact on activity would be lower.

This is because, from the economy<sup>’</sup>s standpoint, the RBI<sup>’</sup>s dividend—to the extent that it is achieved from higher interest on FX reserves (i.e. akin to a foreign transfer) and exchange gains from FX transactions—will not have an contractionary impact on growth, like cutting expenditures or higher taxes.

## Q124 and full year GDP print awaited

India<sup>’</sup>s 1Q GDP print which is to be released on 31<sup>st</sup> May is expected at 7% oya—stronger than the National Statistics Office (NSO) estimate of 5.9% oya. This would raise the 2023-24 full year forecast to 7.9% oya. Like the last print, there is expected to be a large wedge between GVA and GDP with the former expected to close to 6%. Recall that GVA is the better reflection of underlying economy as boost to GDP is technical in nature.

However, there are three key factors to monitor in the upcoming GDP data:

With the estimated foodgrain production for the 2023-24 crop year (July-June) having contracted vs the previous year owing to the poor monsoon, agricultural GDP growth is likely to stay tepid.

On the other hand, an acceleration in IP growth (Figure 3) and resilient earnings growth of manufacturing firms suggest a potential upside surprise in the manufacturing sector.

Weakness in centre<sup>’</sup>s revenue expenditure growth in the months of January and February (-2.5% oya) should translate into lower public administration growth.

Figure 3: Industrial Production (IP)
![](images/d94e6d40ac928d3f1071dec6666be31e62bc8db3fe14c5de356f2779c0cde791.jpg)
Source: MoSPI, J.P. Morgan

## General elections to conclude on June 1st

India<sup>’</sup>s ongoing general elections, which began on 19<sup>th</sup> April, are being conducted in seven phases. Elections for the first five phases, covering 428 seats, have already concluded. This leaves 115 seats to be voted on in the final two phases. The sixth phase will take place on May 25<sup>th</sup>, and the seventh phase on June 1<sup>st</sup>, with exit polls also being announced that night . The vote counting will begin on June 4<sup>th</sup>, with results expected the same day.

## Asia Focus: Tariff impact

The latest tariff announcement from the US on imports of selected Chinese goods has raised concerns of renewed bilateral or multi-lateral trade tensions. The amount of affected Chinese goods is not large, at US\$18bn (4% of Chinese exports to the US in 2023), suggesting likely limited immediate impact. But the market concern is more about whether the tariff will expand to other goods, or spill over to a third country and impact trans-shipments or China<sup>’</sup>s overseas investment.

The affected goods are sub-components of the below categories that the US aims to friend-shore (Table 1). US imports of these broader product categories from China amounted to US\$47bn in 2023. In terms of relative share, PPE and durable medical equipment accounted for 56.8% (US\$16.7bn) in 2023, but affected products in this category (facemasks and medical gloves) were only US\$0.4bn. This is followed by semiconductors and testing and diagnostics. As we have discussed, China has been losing market share in most of these categories in recent years (e.g. solar, semiconductor, natural graphite and magnesite), with the major exception of large capacity batteries. The battery space is also feeling more of the pain from the latest tariff hike (Table 2).

The slack created by China in the US market has been mostly filled by ASEAN in the region. For example, ASEAN<sup>’</sup>s solar market share in the US spiked from 1.9% in 2015-2017 to 39.2% in 2023 (Table 3). This did prompt the US DoC<sup>’</sup>s investigation into whether solar imports from ASEAN included materials sourced from China, circumventing anti-dumping/countervailing tariffs, although China<sup>’</sup>s solar exports are relatively limited (US\$5.1bn in 2023, vs. US solar imports from ASEAN at US\$16.5bn, Tables 4 and 5). In June 2022, the US announced it would not impose new tariffs on solar imports from ASEAN for two years, but concerns of renewed tariff increases may have risen.
